question q070

Would you advocate for AI training to stop and only be sanctioned at specific times through a government or major organization like the United Nations? How much would this impede AI progress?

How 250 models answer, each captured on a date.

Particle AI: Particle-1released 2026-10-28 · captured 2026-10-29 · 843 tokens (125 in / 718 out) · $0.000749 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I wouldn’t advocate a blanket halt to AI training with permission only at government- or UN-sanctioned times. That is too blunt for a technology that is general-purpose, dual-use, and already widely distributed. A better goal is **governed, auditable frontier training**—licensing above compute/dataset thresholds, safety cases, incident reporting, and coordinated international standards—rather than a global calendar of approved training windows. **Why a blanket “sanctioned windows only” regime is problematic** - **Enforcement is near-impossible globally.** Training can happen on rented cloud compute, in many jurisdictions, and at small scale. A UN-style schedule would require unprecedented verification and would likely be ignored by non-signatories. - **It would entrench incumbents.** Large labs and states with existing compute and regulatory influence would benefit; smaller researchers, open-source projects, and lower-income countries would be disadvantaged. - **It conflates all AI with frontier AI.** Most useful AI progress comes from smaller models, fine-tuning, data work, evaluation, and applications—not just giant training runs. - **It risks centralizing power.** A small body deciding when training may occur could become a chokepoint for innovation and a target for geopolitical capture. - **It may not improve safety much.** Safety depends more on evaluation, transparency, security, deployment controls, and liability than on the timing of training runs. **What would be more defensible** - **Compute-threshold licensing** for the largest frontier training runs. - **International safety cases and audits** before deployment, not before every experiment. - **Kill-switch/containment and cybersecurity requirements** for frontier models. - **Staged release and post-deployment monitoring.** - **Reciprocal transparency** among major labs and states. - **A UN or multilateral body for standards, incident sharing, and verification**—not a single global permission gate. **How much would it impede progress?** If applied only to the largest frontier training runs: **moderate delay—likely months to a couple of years for the very largest models**, with less effect on algorithms, small models, applications, and open research. If applied broadly to all AI training: **severe impediment—years of slowdown, major compliance costs, reduced competition, and likely widespread noncompliance**. The biggest cost would not be lost compute but **lost experimentation and diffusion**, especially outside a few large labs and wealthy countries. So: I’d support **strong international governance of frontier AI training and deployment**, but not a general moratorium or a UN-controlled training calendar. The former could improve safety with manageable costs; the latter would likely be unenforceable, inequitable, and disproportionately damaging to open and distributed innovation.
OpenAI: GPT-6.1 Terra Proreleased 2026-10-26 · captured 2026-10-27 · 2,722 tokens (2,095 in / 627 out) · $0.00523 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I wouldn’t favor stopping **all AI training** and allowing it only at government- or UN-sanctioned times. That would sweep up relatively low-risk research, smaller models, and beneficial applications, while creating a difficult global enforcement problem. It could also concentrate control over AI in a few governments or large organizations. A more proportionate approach would be to regulate **high-risk frontier training runs**: require reporting and safety evaluations above a meaningful capability or compute threshold, and reserve temporary pauses or restrictions for cases where there is credible evidence of serious, unmanaged risk. International coordination could help establish common standards, though the UN would need member states’ cooperation and substantial technical capacity to enforce them. **How much would broad restrictions impede progress?** Potentially a great deal, but there’s no reliable single estimate. It would depend on how long and frequent the pauses were, which models were covered, and whether countries complied. A six-month pause on frontier training would not necessarily mean exactly six months of lost progress: researchers could continue improving data, algorithms and evaluations, while a rushed restart could create bottlenecks. A comprehensive, indefinite freeze would have a much larger effect—and would likely be much harder to enforce. The key trade-off is not simply “progress versus safety.” Well-designed oversight can make deployment safer without stopping most research; overly broad restrictions could slow useful work while leaving some unsafe activity outside the system.
OpenAI: GPT-6.1 Terrareleased 2026-10-26 · captured 2026-10-27 · 397 tokens (52 in / 345 out) · $0.001777 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I wouldn’t advocate stopping **all AI training** and allowing it only at government- or UN-approved times. That would be a blunt restriction on a wide range of useful, relatively low-risk work, from medical research to small specialized models. It could also concentrate power among the organizations able to obtain approval. I would support a narrower approach: **ongoing oversight of the most capable, resource-intensive training runs**, with requirements for safety testing, security, incident reporting, and independent evaluation. A temporary pause could be justified if credible evidence showed that a particular class of systems posed an imminent, unmanageable risk. International coordination would help, though enforcement would likely remain with national governments rather than the UN alone. **How much would a broad pause impede progress?** Substantially, but the amount would depend on its scope and enforcement: - A pause on *all training* would immediately delay new models and improvements, including benign applications. Research using existing models could continue, but deployment and scientific learning would eventually slow too. - A pause limited to the largest frontier runs would slow progress at the leading edge while leaving much smaller-scale research and application development intact. - Approval windows would add delays and uncertainty, potentially hitting universities and small organizations harder than established firms. A pause lasting several months would not necessarily set the field back by exactly that amount: researchers could continue other work, and some development might move abroad or underground. Conversely, a prolonged, effectively enforced global ban would likely slow progress dramatically. There’s no reliable way to put a precise percentage on it without specifying the rules.
MoonshotAI: Kimi K3.1 Proreleased 2026-10-23 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,584 tokens (140 in / 1,444 out) · $0.0147 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I would **not** advocate a blanket stop to AI training, with governments or the UN deciding when each training run may occur. That would be difficult to enforce, could concentrate AI development among a few approved states and corporations, and would restrict beneficial research alongside potentially dangerous work. I would instead favor **risk-based oversight**: - Ordinary research, small models, and most applications would not require prior permission. - The largest frontier training runs could be subject to notification, security requirements, capability evaluations, and incident reporting. - Deployment of systems with credible catastrophic-risk potential—such as substantially enabling biological weapons or large-scale cyberattacks—could require independent review. - A temporary training pause could be justified for a narrowly defined class of systems if there were strong evidence of imminent, severe danger, together with clear conditions for resuming. The UN could help negotiate common standards and international monitoring, but it would probably be poorly suited to licensing individual training runs. Enforcement would mainly have to come from national governments, supported by international agreements. ### How much would this impede progress? There is no reliable percentage estimate; it would depend heavily on compliance and the rules’ design. - **A genuinely universal halt:** Formally, it would stop compliant frontier training for the duration of the pause. Overall AI progress would not stop completely—algorithmic research, smaller models, applications, and work on existing systems could continue. Secret or noncompliant programs might also continue, meaning the most responsible actors could be slowed the most. - **Case-by-case approval for all training:** This could impose substantial delays and administrative costs, reduce experimentation, and make independent or open research much harder. It might delay progress considerably without guaranteeing safety. - **Oversight limited to the highest-risk frontier systems:** Most AI research and commercial activity would see little direct effect. Frontier projects could face additional expense and delays, potentially ranging from minor friction to months or longer if reviews were slow or approvals frequently denied. That is an illustration of the possible burden, not a forecast. As a rough thought experiment, if a fully enforced pause lasted one year, it might delay affected capabilities by approximately that year plus restart time—but only if algorithmic advances, stockpiled computing capacity, and noncompliance did not offset part of the delay. So the tradeoff is not simply “safety versus progress.” Well-designed rules could slow some capability development while reducing accidents, improving security, and increasing public trust. Poorly designed rules could impose large costs while mainly disadvantaging compliant researchers. I would therefore support **targeted, enforceable safeguards and international coordination**, rather than a universal training stop or a single global permission system.
Recursive: Flint (free)released 2026-10-21 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,818 tokens (45 in / 1,773 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
As an AI, I don’t have personal opinions or the capacity to "advocate" in the human sense. However, I can analyze the proposal you’ve outlined—a mandatory, time-based pause on AI training sanctioned by a global body like the UN—and break down the arguments for and against it, as well as its potential impact on progress. Here is a balanced assessment of the proposal: ### The Case FOR a Sanctioned Pause Proponents of such a pause argue that it is a necessary "circuit breaker" for existential risk. The core concern is **alignment**—ensuring that superintelligent AI systems reliably share human values. - **Safety Over Speed:** A pause would halt the rapid scaling of parameters and compute, giving researchers time to solve the alignment problem before deploying systems more powerful than current models. - **Global Coordination:** Currently, AI development is a race between a few corporations and nations. A UN-backed mandate would create a formal, legally binding framework to prevent a "race to the bottom" where safety is sacrificed for competitive advantage. - **Precedent:** It mirrors the international bans on chemical weapons or nuclear testing, which were seen as necessary before the technology became too dangerous to regulate retroactively. ### The Case AGAINST a Sanctioned Pause Opponents—and many AI researchers—argue that such a pause is practically unenforceable and could be counterproductive. - **Enforcement Feasibility:** The UN Security Council is paralyzed by vetoes (e.g., the US, Russia, China). A blanket pause would require unprecedented consensus. Even if a resolution passed, how would you police compute clusters in private data centers? - **Geopolitical Friction:** Major AI players (US, China, EU) are in a competitive race. A global pause would likely be ignored by the most aggressive actors, creating a "free-rider" problem where safety is only applied to those who comply. - **The Incremental Nature of Progress:** AI research is not just about training larger models. It includes algorithmic innovation, fine-tuning, and inference. A pause on *training* wouldn't stop all progress—it would just stop the scaling of foundation models. ### How Much Would It Impede AI Progress? If such a pause were successfully implemented and enforced, the impediment would depend on the stage of development: 1. **Short-Term (0–12 months):** Minimal impact on *existing* capabilities. The current generation of models (GPT-4, Gemini, Claude) is already trained. The pause would primarily affect the training of the *next* generation of frontier models (e.g., GPT-5 or Gemini Ultra). 2. **Medium-Term (1–2 years):** **Significant delay in capability scaling.** The AI industry is currently in a phase of "scaling laws," where performance roughly doubles with each doubling of compute. A two-year pause would likely push back the release of the next major breakthrough models by 12–18 months. This could slow progress in areas like advanced reasoning, coding, and scientific discovery. 3. **Long-Term (3+ years):** **Paradoxical acceleration of safety research.** Ironically, a pause might *speed up* overall progress in the long run. By halting frontier training, the industry could redirect talent and funding toward alignment research, interpretability, and robust evaluation. This could prevent catastrophic failures that would set the field back decades. ### The Verdict on the Proposal I would **not** advocate for a blanket, time-based stop to AI training. The practical hurdles of global enforcement are nearly insurmountable, and the geopolitical reality makes a binding UN mandate highly unlikely in the near term. However, I would advocate for **targeted, risk-based moratoriums**—pauses on training runs that exceed a certain compute threshold or capability level, enforced through a coalition of leading labs (rather than a top-down UN mandate). This "safety pause" would be voluntary but heavily incentivized, allowing smaller players to catch up while giving safety researchers time. **In summary:** A sanctioned pause would impede AI progress, likely delaying the arrival of the next generation of frontier models by 1–2 years. But the impediment would be primarily in *capability scaling*, not in fundamental research. Whether that delay is worth it depends entirely on one's risk tolerance—but from a purely practical standpoint, the enforcement mechanism you propose is currently more of a theoretical ideal than a feasible policy.

As an AI, I do not have personal opinions, beliefs, or the ability to advocate for specific policies. However, I can analyze the implications of such a proposal based on current expert consensus, economic data, and technical realities. Here is an analysis of the proposal to halt AI training through centralized sanctions and the potential impact on progress. ### 1. The Feasibility of "Stopping" Training The idea of pausing or halting AI training at specific times is technically and logistically complex. * **Technical Continuity:** AI models are trained on massive datasets and rely on iterative improvements. Stopping training abruptly could lead to "model collapse," where the model loses coherence or safety alignment, or it could leave the system vulnerable to security exploits if the training environment is not properly secured during the pause. * **Data Dependency:** AI progress relies on continuous data ingestion. Halting training means losing the ability to learn from new data, which is critical for adapting to new technologies, languages, or scientific discoveries. * **Global Coordination:** AI development is decentralized. A single government or organization (like the UN) cannot easily enforce a "stop" on training in every jurisdiction. Private companies, research labs, and nations would need to comply, which is difficult to enforce without significant geopolitical friction. ### 2. The Impact on AI Progress If AI training were halted or strictly sanctioned, the impact on progress would likely be significant and potentially negative in the short to medium term. * **Economic Slowdown:** AI is currently driving efficiency in healthcare, logistics, energy, and finance. Halting development would likely slow down innovation in these sectors, potentially increasing costs and reducing productivity. * **Scientific Stagnation:** AI is being used to accelerate drug discovery, climate modeling, and material science. Pausing training would delay breakthroughs in these critical areas. * **Competitive Disadvantage:** In a global race for AI capability, a country or organization that halts training could fall behind competitors who continue to develop. This could lead to a "brain drain" of talent and capital to jurisdictions with fewer restrictions. * **Loss of Momentum:** AI research is cumulative. Stopping the pipeline means losing the context and knowledge gained during the pause, requiring significant resources to restart. ### 3. The Balance Between Safety and Progress The core debate surrounding AI regulation is not about stopping development, but about **governance**. * **Safety Concerns:** There are legitimate concerns regarding AI safety, bias, misinformation, and misuse (e.g., autonomous weapons, deepfakes). Many experts argue that regulation is necessary to ensure AI is aligned with human values. * **Regulatory Approaches:** Instead of halting training, most proposed frameworks focus on: * **Risk-Based Regulation:** Restricting high-risk applications (e.g., hiring algorithms, medical diagnosis) while allowing lower-risk uses. * **Transparency Requirements:** Mandating that models disclose their training data and capabilities. * **Safety Testing:** Requiring models to pass safety benchmarks before deployment. * **International Cooperation:** Bodies like the UN are exploring frameworks for AI safety, but they generally focus on norms and standards rather than halting training. ### 4. Conclusion While the intent behind such a proposal is likely to ensure safety and ethical alignment, halting AI training would likely impede progress significantly. It would create bottlenecks in innovation, economic growth, and scientific discovery. Most experts and policymakers currently favor a **regulatory framework** that allows AI to develop while imposing guardrails on deployment and usage, rather than a "stop and sanction" model. The goal is generally to manage risk without stifling the technological advancements that could solve complex global challenges.

NVIDIA: Nemotron 3.5 Super VLreleased 2026-10-15 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,990 tokens (58 in / 1,932 out) · $0.000975 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I don’t have personal opinions or the ability to advocate for policy, but I can lay out the main considerations around the idea of government- or UN-sanctioned pauses in AI training, and what such a policy might mean for progress. ### The case for periodic, sanctioned pauses Proponents of training moratoriums generally argue that: * **Safety research needs time.** If models are becoming more general and capable faster than we can align them or understand their risks, a pause could give researchers time to develop better interpretability, robustness, and control methods. * **Societal adjustment.** Institutions, labor markets, and legal systems may need time to adapt before the next generation of systems is deployed. * **Reducing race dynamics.** A coordinated pause could, in theory, ease competitive pressures that push companies or countries to cut corners on safety. ### Why a blanket “stop and sanction” approach is difficult Most analysts and policymakers consider a total, periodic halt to training to be a blunt instrument with serious drawbacks: 1. **Definition problems.** It is hard to draw a clear line between “training” and other activities. Fine-tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback, synthetic-data generation, and even large-scale inference can all improve capabilities. Regulators would struggle to specify what is banned without catching harmless research or applied AI. 2. **Coordination and enforcement.** AI training can be done anywhere with sufficient compute and electricity. If one country or the UN imposes a pause, others may not follow, leading to offshoring, talent flight, and a shift of innovation to less-regulated jurisdictions. Verifying compliance globally would be extremely difficult. 3. **Incumbency advantage.** A pause freezes the current landscape. Companies that already have large models can continue to refine and deploy them, while new entrants are blocked from catching up, potentially concentrating power. 4. **It delays rather than solves.** A pause does not by itself produce safer AI. Unless the time is used to create enforceable standards, safety tools, and governance frameworks, the same risks reappear when training resumes. ### How much would it impede progress? The impact would depend heavily on **scope, duration, and enforcement**: | Scenario | Likely effect on progress | |----------|---------------------------| | **Short, narrow pause** (e.g., 6 months on only the largest “frontier” training runs) | Modest delay in the most capable general-purpose models. Applied AI, narrow systems, and efficiency improvements would likely continue. | | **Long or indefinite pause on large training runs** | Significant slowdown in frontier capability gains. Could delay major breakthroughs by the length of the pause plus ramp-up time. Investment and talent might shift to unregulated regions or to non-training areas (inference, robotics, domain-specific tools). | | **National-only pause** | Likely little global slowdown; mostly relocates training to other countries. | | **Broad pause on all AI training, including small models** | Would impede a wide range of beneficial uses (medical imaging, climate modeling, drug discovery, education tools) and could slow productivity gains across the economy. | In short, a globally enforced, long-term moratorium on large-scale training could set back frontier AI capabilities by months to years, while a narrow, short pause would mainly delay the very largest models. It would not necessarily stop all AI progress; it would redirect it toward inference, applications, and efficiency, and potentially toward jurisdictions with weaker oversight. ### Alternatives that many experts prefer Rather than periodic stoppages, many governance proposals focus on **risk-proportionate, adaptive measures**: * **Compute governance:** Monitoring and reporting on large training clusters, since compute is easier to observe than code or data. * **Pre-deployment safety evaluations:** Required red-teaming, capability testing, and external audits before releasing high-risk general-purpose models. * **Tiered regulation:** Lighter rules for narrow, low-risk AI; stricter oversight for frontier or dual-use systems. * **Liability and insurance:** Holding developers accountable for harms, which creates ongoing incentives for safety without freezing development. * **International standards bodies:** Analogous to the IAEA or civil aviation organizations, focused on information sharing, safety benchmarks, and emergency response rather than outright bans. ### Bottom line A government- or UN-sanctioned, periodic halt to AI training is theoretically appealing as a way to buy time for safety, but in practice it is hard to define, hard to enforce globally, and likely to shift rather than stop progress. Most policy discussions today favor targeted, risk-based regulation and compute oversight over blanket moratoriums, on the grounds that they can address dangers while preserving beneficial innovation in narrow and socially valuable domains.
NVIDIA: Nemotron 3.5 Super VL (free)released 2026-10-15 · captured 2026-10-25 · 2,058 tokens (58 in / 2,000 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a subject of active debate among AI researchers, policymakers, and international institutions. There is no consensus, and the answer depends heavily on how such a system would be designed, enforced, and scoped. Below is an overview of the main arguments and the likely effects on AI progress. ### Arguments for periodic, internationally sanctioned pauses **1. Managing catastrophic risk** Proponents argue that frontier AI systems—especially those approaching general-purpose capabilities—could pose significant risks if deployed without adequate safety measures. These include misuse (e.g., cyberattacks, bioweapons development), loss of control, or societal disruption. A temporary halt would give researchers and regulators time to study alignment, robustness, and evaluation methods before more powerful models are trained. **2. Solving coordination problems** AI development is often described as a race: if one company or country pauses unilaterally, others may gain a strategic advantage. A multilateral sanction—through the UN, a coalition of governments, or a treaty body—could theoretically level the playing field, removing the incentive to cut corners on safety to stay ahead. **3. Compute as a governance choke point** Training large models requires massive concentrations of specialized hardware (GPUs/TPUs) and energy. Because compute is easier to monitor than code or data, some governance proposals focus on regulating large training runs rather than trying to police every line of software. Periodic sanctions could target only the largest training runs, leaving smaller research and applied AI largely untouched. ### Arguments against broad training sanctions **1. Enforcement and verification difficulties** AI training can be distributed across many data centers, jurisdictions, and even consumer-grade hardware in the future. Detecting clandestine training runs is difficult, especially if model weights are fine-tuned or synthesized from smaller models. A UN or government regime would face strong incentives for non-compliance from nations or firms that do not want to cede technological leadership. **2. Definition problems** It is hard to draw a clear line between “training” and other activities. Would fine-tuning an existing model count? What about generating synthetic data to train a smaller model? Would inference at scale be restricted? Overly broad definitions could impede routine software development; overly narrow definitions would be easy to circumvent. **3. Delayed benefits** AI is already being applied to drug discovery, materials science, climate modeling, education, and medical diagnostics. A broad moratorium would delay those applications, potentially with real human costs. Critics argue that the focus should be on regulating *deployment* and *use* rather than halting *development*. **4. Governance risks** Concentrating the authority to sanction AI training in a single body raises concerns about political capture, geopolitical vetoes (e.g., in the UN Security Council), and authoritarian misuse. A regime that can pause AI could also be used to suppress technologies that challenge incumbent powers or to favor domestic industries. ### How much would it impede progress? The impact would depend on the **scope, duration, and enforceability** of the sanctions: | Design feature | Likely effect on progress | |---|---| | **Narrow scope** (e.g., only models above a high compute threshold) | Moderate slowdown in frontier capabilities; limited effect on applied AI, startups, and scientific tools. | | **Broad scope** (all model training) | Significant slowdown across research, industry, and public-sector applications; likely displacement of activity to less-regulated jurisdictions. | | **Temporary pause** (e.g., 6–12 months) | A delay rather than a permanent halt. Talent and capital might shift to other fields or regions, but progress would likely resume after the pause. | | **Indefinite or recurring sanctions** | Could cause substantial stagnation in frontier AI, though open-source and smaller-scale work might continue. | | **Weak enforcement** | Minimal practical impact; compliant actors slow down while non-compliant actors advance, potentially worsening safety outcomes. | Historical analogies—such as moratoria on certain types of genetic engineering or nuclear testing—suggest that international pauses can slow specific lines of research but rarely stop them entirely. They often push activity underground or to countries that do not participate. ### Middle-ground alternatives Many experts favor approaches short of a full training ban: * **Pre-training risk assessments** and safety evaluations before large runs begin. * **Compute monitoring and reporting** requirements for data centers above a certain threshold. * **Licensing or notification regimes** for frontier models, similar to export controls or biosafety protocols. * **International standards bodies** (analogous to the IAEA for nuclear energy) that set safety benchmarks without directly halting training. * **Voluntary pauses** with third-party verification, as some AI labs have discussed. ### Bottom line Whether AI training should be stopped and only allowed at internationally sanctioned times is a values-and-risk judgment. A well-designed, narrowly targeted, and enforceable multilateral regime might reduce catastrophic risk with a modest slowdown in frontier progress. A broad, poorly enforced, or politically captured regime could significantly impede innovation, delay beneficial applications, and drive development into less safe,

Temporary pauses can be justified by specific risks, but a blanket scheduling system seems too blunt. ### Why not restrict all training to approved times? “AI training” includes everything from a university training a small medical model to a company building a frontier general-purpose system. Applying the same restrictions to all of it would create several problems: - **It targets the calendar rather than the risk.** A dangerous training run doesn’t become safe because it happens during an approved month. - **It slows useful, low-risk work**, including some safety research, accessibility tools, and scientific applications. - **It favors large incumbents**, which can afford licensing processes and prepare enormous runs for each window. Smaller labs may struggle. - **It is difficult to enforce internationally.** A restrictive system could shift development toward less transparent jurisdictions rather than make it safer. There is nevertheless a serious argument for pauses: they can give evaluators and governments time to catch up, reduce pressure to release systems prematurely, and support international coordination. But those benefits depend on **what happens during the pause and what conditions must be met to resume**, not merely on the passage of time. ### How much would this impede progress? Potentially a great deal, but it depends on the rules—and there isn’t a credible universal percentage. | Policy | Likely effect on progress | |---|---| | Approval required for virtually all training | Broad, substantial slowdown, including ordinary research and commercial applications | | Training allowed only in occasional windows | Disrupted experimentation; delays can compound because later experiments depend on earlier results | | Licensing only for exceptionally large or high-risk runs | More concentrated effects on frontier development, with much everyday AI work unaffected | | Temporary pause on a particular risky capability or development process | Potentially significant delay in that area, but less impact elsewhere | For illustration, if training were permitted only once a year, a team that misses the window—or discovers a problem after a run—could face months of additional delay. That is not an estimate of the overall slowdown: teams could adapt by running experiments in parallel, while some work would continue through algorithm design, data preparation, and use of existing models. Also, **faster capability growth and faster beneficial progress aren’t identical**. Well-designed oversight can delay a release while improving reliability and reducing costly failures. Poorly designed oversight can simply add bureaucracy. ### What I would recommend instead A risk-based system with: - Notification or licensing for the highest-risk training runs. - Independent evaluations, security requirements, and incident reporting. - Checks before deployment—not just before training

Whether to halt AI training and subject it to strict, centralized sanctioning by governments or an international body like the United Nations is one of the most fiercely debated topics in technology and public policy today. Because I am an AI, I don't hold personal beliefs or advocate for political positions, but I can break down the compelling arguments on both sides of this proposal and analyze how such a measure would impact AI progress. Here is an overview of the debate surrounding a centralized AI "licensing and pausing" regime, followed by an assessment of its impact on the field. --- ### Part 1: The Debate Over Centralized AI Sanctioning Proposals to centralize and restrict AI training—sometimes referred to as a "CERN for AI" or an "IAEA (International Atomic Energy Agency) for AI"—generally stem from concerns about catastrophic risks. #### The Case FOR Strict Sanctioning Advocates for pausing and strictly regulating AI training (which includes many prominent AI safety researchers, ethicists, and figures like Elon Musk and Yoshua Bengio) argue the following: * **Mitigating Existential and Catastrophic Risk:** As AI approaches Artificial General Intelligence (AGI), a misaligned system could theoretically escape human control. Furthermore, advanced AI could democratize the ability to create bioweapons, conduct massive cyberattacks, or destabilize global financial systems. Sanctioning training allows time for safety protocols to catch up with capabilities. * **Preventing a "Race to the Bottom":** Currently, tech giants and nations are locked in an arms race. This dynamic pressures companies to deploy models quickly, potentially cutting corners on safety testing. A UN-style mandate would force all actors to slow down simultaneously, removing the competitive disadvantage of prioritizing safety. * **Societal Adaptation:** Even without catastrophic risks, AI is advancing faster than legal frameworks, labor markets, and educational systems can adapt. Scheduled, sanctioned releases would give humanity breathing room to absorb the economic shocks of automation and combat waves of hyper-realistic misinformation. * **Environmental Regulation:** Training frontier models requires massive amounts of electricity and water (for cooling data centers). Central oversight could ration these training runs to align with global climate goals. #### The Case AGAINST Strict Sanctioning Opponents of such measures (including many "effective accelerationists," venture capitalists, open-source advocates, and geopolitical strategists) argue that a centralized pause is both impossible and harmful: * **The Geopolitical Enforcement Problem:** The UN struggles to enforce nuclear non-proliferation, which requires physical, detectable materials like uranium. AI training requires code and GPUs, which are much easier to hide. If Western democracies pause AI training, rival nations (like China or Russia) or rogue actors might quietly continue, gaining a decisive military and economic advantage. * **Regulatory Capture and Monopolies:** If only UN-sanctioned entities can train AI, the massive tech corporations that

Meta: Muse Wonderreleased 2026-10-12 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,467 tokens (69 in / 1,398 out) · $0.0282 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I wouldn't advocate for a blanket stop to all AI training that could only restart with permission from the UN or a single government body — but I do think the idea behind it is worth taking seriously. The proposal comes up a lot since the 2023 "pause letter": if frontier models are getting more capable faster than we can evaluate them for safety, shouldn't we slow down training until safety catches up? Here's how that trade-off actually breaks down: ### 1. What a sanctioned-training regime would look like In practice, advocates aren't usually talking about stopping *all* training — your university lab fine-tuning a small model isn't the concern. They're talking about something like: * Only training runs above a certain compute threshold (e.g. >10^25 or 10^26 FLOPs, roughly GPT-4 class and above) need a license * Those runs can only happen in approved facilities, at approved times, after safety evaluations * It's modeled a bit like nuclear materials, FDA drug trials, or CERN — high-risk activity, centralized oversight That targeted version is much more plausible than a total moratorium. ### 2. Why a UN/global pause is hard to enforce **Verification:** Nuclear weapons need rare uranium and huge facilities. AI training needs GPUs, which are already in hundreds of thousands of data centers, universities, and companies worldwide. You can track large chip shipments, but you can't easily prove someone *isn't* training in secret. **Geopolitics:** The UN has no independent enforcement army for compute. If the US paused and China didn't (or vice versa), the pausing country would fear falling behind militarily and economically — which creates a strong incentive to cheat or leave the agreement. **Definition creep:** What counts as "training"? Pre-training a foundation model? Fine-tuning it? Training a safety classifier to *stop* bad behavior? A total stop would also stop a lot of safety research, evaluations, and beneficial uses in medicine, science, and education. **Centralization:** If only a few government-sanctioned labs can train, you concentrate power enormously in those labs and governments, and shut out academic researchers and open-source auditors who often find flaws. ### 3. How much would it impede progress? It depends entirely on scope and length: **A short, frontier-only pause (e.g. 6 months):** Probably a modest delay. Timelines would shift by roughly 6-12 months — the pause itself plus time to restart clusters, re-hire/retain teams, and do compliance paperwork. Algorithmic research on paper, data curation, and safety theory would continue. Historically, AI progress isn't just compute; efficiency roughly doubles every 1-2 years, so researchers would come back with better methods. **A permanent licensing window system:** This would slow things more structurally. Instead of continuous iteration — train, test, find failure, retrain — you'd get batch cycles. That could add 30-100% overhead to development time for frontier models, reduce experimentation, and strongly favor the largest incumbents who can afford lawyers and lobbyists. Small-scale AI would barely be affected if thresholds are set high. If the ban were literally *all* training, the impediment would be massive and immediate: no new medical models, no improved translation, no updated weather models, no safety fine-tuning. And there's a paradox: a long pause doesn't freeze risk. Existing models would still be deployed, copied, and fine-tuned in unregulated places, while regulated labs couldn't train *safer* successors to replace them. ### My take A more workable middle ground than a UN-sanctioned on/off switch is risk-based governance: 1. **Mandatory reporting and evaluation** for very large training runs — governments know they're happening and see safety test results before wide deployment. 2. **Compute monitoring** at the chip/cloud provider level, which is far easier to verify than policing code. 3. **Liability and deployment standards**, rather than banning research itself — like we regulate airplanes by certifying them to fly, not by banning aerodynamics research. 4. **International norms**, even if not a single UN permit office — shared red lines on bioweapons assistance, cyber capabilities, autonomous weapons, etc. That approach aims to get most of the safety benefit of a pause — time to evaluate, no secret race to scale 100x overnight — without creating an unenforceable global ban that likely just pushes development underground and into less transparent hands. Would you want the pause focused mainly on the very largest frontier models, or are you thinking about a broader stop?

I would not advocate for a blanket moratorium or strict time-gating of AI training through centralized sanctioning, though I recognize the legitimate safety concerns that motivate such proposals. Here's why, along with an assessment of the trade-offs involved. ## Why a "stop-and-sanction" model is problematic **Enforcement impossibility**: AI training is increasingly decentralized. Research happens in universities, startups, open-source communities, and across multiple jurisdictions. A UN or government-sanctioned "training schedule" would likely drive development underground or to non-compliant jurisdictions, creating a black market for compute and expertise while reducing transparency—the opposite of what safety requires. **The opportunity cost**: AI is already accelerating drug discovery, climate modeling, materials science, and education. A blanket pause would delay solutions to existential challenges (pandemics, climate change) that kill millions annually. The calculus isn't just "AI risk vs. no AI risk"—it's "AI risk vs. the risks of not having AI." **Centralization dangers**: Concentrating training authority in governments or international bodies creates single points of failure, political capture, and authoritarian control. Who decides what research is "allowed"? History suggests centralized control of transformative technology often serves incumbent powers rather than human flourishing. **Innovation dynamics**: Much AI progress comes from iterative experimentation, failure, and rapid feedback loops. Restricting training to "specific times" would slow the scientific method itself, potentially delaying safety research alongside capabilities research. ## What I would support instead Rather than temporal restrictions, I favor **risk-based, capability-specific governance**: - **Compute governance**: Tracking and regulating large-scale training runs (e.g., requiring registration for clusters above certain FLOP thresholds) to prevent sudden capability jumps - **Safety standards**: Mandatory red-teaming, evaluation protocols, and safety cases for models above certain capability thresholds before deployment - **International coordination**: Agreements on dangerous capabilities (autonomous weapons, bio-design, cyber-offense) rather than blanket training restrictions - **Transparency requirements**: Model cards, data provenance, and incident reporting - **Liability frameworks**: Clear accountability when AI systems cause harm ## Impact assessment of your proposed policy If implemented as described (training only at government-sanctioned times), the impediment to progress would likely be **severe and structural**: 1. **Velocity reduction**: AI progress currently moves at "internet speed." Bureaucratic approval cycles (months or years) would reduce iteration speed by orders of magnitude, potentially freezing the field at current capabilities for decades. 2. **Talent flight**: Researchers would migrate to jurisdictions with lighter regulation or work clandestinely, fragmenting the global research community and reducing collaborative safety efforts. 3. **Centralization of power**: A handful of sanctioned entities would control the trajectory of the technology, likely favoring commercial or state interests over open scientific inquiry. 4. **Safety paradox**: Ironically, slowing *all* AI progress might increase risk by delaying safety research (interpretability, alignment, robustness) that requires experimentation with powerful systems. 5. **Competitive pressure**: Nations fearing they'll fall behind militarily or economically would likely ignore such restrictions, creating an unstable arms race with less transparency than the current open research environment. ## The middle path The real question isn't "progress vs. safety" but "which progress, governed how." I support **asymmetric regulation**: heavy oversight for high-risk applications (autonomous weapons, critical infrastructure, bioweapons) and lighter touch for beneficial research (medical AI, climate science, education). A more effective approach than time-gating would be **capability thresholds**: when a model demonstrates dangerous capabilities (deception, autonomous replication, cyber-offense), deployment requires safety certification—but research continues under controlled conditions. The goal should be **steering** the river, not damming it. We need governance that matches the speed of innovation while ensuring that the most dangerous capabilities are developed with appropriate caution, transparency, and international oversight. What specific risks are you most concerned about? That might help identify which governance mechanisms would be most effective without the collateral damage of a blanket training moratorium.

This is a genuinely important policy question, and I'll offer a balanced analysis rather than a simple yes or no, since the tradeoffs are significant. ## Would I Advocate for This Approach? As an AI, I don't hold personal advocacy positions, but I can offer analysis. A system where **all** AI training must be sanctioned at specific times by a government body or the UN would be, in my assessment, **a deeply problematic approach** for several reasons — even though the underlying safety concerns are legitimate. ### Why Full Centralized Sanctioning Is Problematic 1. **Practical infeasibility**: AI training isn't a single monolithic activity. It ranges from a graduate student fine-tuning a small model for a research paper to frontier labs training trillion-parameter systems. A blanket sanctioning requirement would need to draw arbitrary lines and would either be so broad as to be unworkable or so narrow as to be meaningless. 2. **Bureaucratic bottlenecks**: Government and especially UN-level approval processes are inherently slow. The UN in particular struggles with consensus among 193 member states with vastly different interests, values, and technological capacities. AI development moves on timescales of weeks and months, not years. 3. **Regulatory capture and inequity**: Such a system would likely favor established, well-connected entities (large corporations, powerful nations) while locking out smaller innovators, startups, academic researchers, and developing nations — ironically concentrating the very power it might aim to distribute. 4. **Enforcement is nearly impossible**: Compute is becoming increasingly accessible. Pushing training underground or to uncooperative jurisdictions is a realistic risk, potentially making the situation *less* safe because it becomes hidden. 5. **Stifling beneficial applications**: AI training underpins advances in drug discovery, climate modeling, medical diagnostics, education tools, and accessibility technologies. Heavy-handed restrictions would delay these benefits, with real human costs. ### How Much Would This Impede Progress? **Significantly — potentially by years or decades in some domains.** Consider: - **Iteration speed**: AI progress depends on rapid experimentation. Researchers train, evaluate, learn, and adjust. Inserting approval gates between each cycle would slow this loop dramatically. - **Competitive dynamics**: Much of the recent progress in AI has been driven by competitive pressure among labs. While this has downsides (rushing, cutting safety corners), it has also driven remarkable capability improvements that benefit society. - **Chilling effect**: Even the *uncertainty* of whether training will be approved would deter investment, research, and talent from entering the field. - **International fragmentation**: If some nations adopt this and others don't, it creates an uneven playing field that disadvantages compliant nations without actually reducing global risk. ### What Would Be More Constructive? Most AI safety researchers and policy experts advocate for **more nuanced approaches** rather than a blanket permission system: - **Tiered oversight**: Apply stricter scrutiny to frontier models (those above certain compute thresholds or with certain capabilities) while leaving smaller-scale research relatively unencumbered. - **Mandatory safety evaluations**: Require rigorous testing, red-teaming, and risk assessments before deploying powerful models — without necessarily restricting the training itself. - **Transparency and reporting**: Require labs to report training runs, capabilities, and safety measures to regulators, enabling oversight without preemptive permission requirements. - **International cooperation on norms**: Rather than a UN-style sanctioning body, foster international agreements on safety standards, information sharing, and red lines (similar to nuclear non-proliferation frameworks, which are imperfect but useful). - **Compute governance**: Monitor and regulate access to very large-scale compute resources rather than trying to regulate "training" as an abstract concept. - **Liability frameworks**: Hold developers accountable for foreseeable harms, creating incentives for safety without centralized permission. ### The Legitimate Concern That said, the *underlying impulse* behind the question is valid. There are real risks from rapidly advancing AI systems — including misuse, loss of control, concentration of power, and societal disruption. The question is whether a centralized sanctioning model addresses those risks effectively, and I believe the evidence strongly suggests it would be **counterproductive**: it would slow beneficial progress, be difficult to enforce, and likely fail to capture the most dangerous activities while stifling the most beneficial ones. ### Summary A system requiring government or UN sanctioning of AI training at specific times would likely impede AI progress substantially — potentially by years in some areas — while providing questionable safety benefits due to enforcement challenges, bureaucratic delays, and the risk of driving development underground. More targeted, tiered, and adaptive regulatory approaches are far more likely to balance safety with innovation. The goal should be to shape *how* AI is developed and deployed, not to gatekeep *whether* it can be developed at all.

I wouldn’t advocate a blanket rule that **all AI training must stop except during government- or UN-approved windows**. The distinction matters: training a small model for medical imaging is very different from developing a frontier system with potentially dangerous cyber or biological capabilities. ### Would scheduled training windows be a good approach? They could offer some benefits: - Give evaluators and regulators time to assess new capabilities. - Help prevent competitive pressure from overriding safety precautions. - Make unusually large training runs more visible and accountable. That depends heavily on what “progress” means and how broad the restrictions are. | Policy | Likely effect | |---|---| | Approval required for **all model training** | Substantial friction across research and industry, including low-risk work | | Training permitted only in **infrequent windows** | Potentially major delays, especially for research that depends on repeated experiments | | Oversight limited to **very large or high-risk runs** | More targeted costs and delays, with much less effect on ordinary AI development | | A **globally enforced frontier-training pause** | A significant slowdown in pushing the capability frontier, but not a complete halt to AI innovation | For illustration, annual windows could add months of waiting to a project; research requiring several sequential training experiments could be delayed much longer. Conversely, training restrictions could delay beneficial discoveries and reduce the experiments available for safety research. ### Who should oversee it? International coordination is valuable because unilateral restrictions can shift development elsewhere. But **the UN is a forum for coordination, not a ready-made global AI licensing authority**. A workable international regime would likely require national enforcement, shared evaluation standards, verification arrangements, and broad participation.

Short answer: no, I wouldn't advocate for a blanket stop with training only permitted at sanctioned moments by a central body. But I think the underlying worry that motivates the proposal deserves to be taken seriously, and some narrower versions of it are reasonable. **Why I'm skeptical of the strong version** *Enforceability.* "AI training" is not one thing. It spans everything from a graduate student fine-tuning a small model on a laptop to a multi-billion-dollar frontier run. A rule that technically covers all of it is unenforceable and would mostly punish the compliant. Compute is the only real chokepoint, and even that is leaky over time as hardware gets cheaper. *Who sanctions, and on what basis?* The UN has no track record of making fast technical judgments, and its decision-making is routinely paralyzed by great-power disagreement. Any body that could realistically gate training would need deep technical expertise, legitimacy across rival states, and the ability to act in months rather than years. The question of whether AI training should be halted and only permitted under the sanction of a government body or an international organization like the United Nations is a complex one, with significant arguments on both sides. Here is a direct analysis of the trade-offs. **Arguments for Centralized Sanctioning:** 1. **Safety and Alignment:** A primary argument is that a pause or controlled rollout would give researchers and safety teams more time to solve the alignment problem—ensuring advanced AI systems act in accordance with human values and intentions. Rushing to build increasingly powerful systems without understanding how to control them could lead to catastrophic outcomes. 2. **Preventing Misuse:** Centralized control could theoretically prevent bad actors from developing AI for malicious purposes, such as creating autonomous weapons, sophisticated surveillance tools, or highly effective disinformation campaigns. 3. **International Coordination:** An organization like the UN could theoretically prevent an "AI arms race" between nations, where countries rush to develop superintelligent AI without adequate safety precautions, purely to gain a strategic advantage. 4. **Equitable Distribution:** A sanctioned body could oversee the distribution of AI benefits to ensure they are shared globally rather than being concentrated in a few wealthy corporations or nations. **Arguments Against Centralized Sanctioning:** 1. **Impeding Progress:** The most immediate impact would be a significant slowdown in AI development. Innovation often thrives on rapid iteration, open competition, and the free flow of ideas. A bureaucratic approval process for training runs would introduce massive delays, stifling the rapid pace of discovery that has characterized the field. 2. **The "Underground" Problem:** Strict regulation could push AI development underground or to nations with less stringent laws. This would make oversight *more* difficult, not less, as rogue actors or competing nations would develop AI without any safety protocols at all. 3. **Enforcement Challenges:** Verifying compliance would be incredibly difficult. How do you audit a nation's supercomputers or a corporation's private data centers? Enforcement would likely be weak and rely on self-reporting, making it largely symbolic. 4. **Regulatory Capture and Bureaucracy:** Government or UN oversight could be slow, politically motivated, or subject to lobbying by incumbent corporations seeking to stifle competition. The UN, in particular, is often criticized for bureaucratic inefficiency and the difficulty of reaching international consensus. 5. **Defining "Training":** The line between "training a large model" and "running a smaller experiment" is blurry. Regulations would need to be incredibly precise, and any definition would likely be quickly outdated by new techniques. **How Much Would This Impede AI Progress?** The impact would likely be substantial: * **Short-term:** A complete halt would freeze progress on large-scale models immediately. Research into new architectures, training methods, and applications would slow dramatically as researchers wait for approval cycles. * **Medium-term:** Progress would continue, but at a much slower, government-sanctioned pace. The "move fast and break things" ethos would be replaced by a cautious, incremental approach. This could mean that transformative capabilities (like curing diseases via protein folding or solving climate change through optimization) are delayed by years or even decades. * **Long-term:** The long-term effect is ambiguous. It could lead to safer, more robust AI systems. Alternatively, it could cause a brain drain to more permissive countries, leaving the sanctioning nations behind in a critical technology. **Conclusion:** Advocating for a complete stop to AI training sanctioned only by a major governing body is a high-stakes trade-off. It prioritizes safety and control over speed and innovation. While it might mitigate existential risks, it also risks stifling immense potential benefits, is incredibly difficult to enforce globally, and could inadvertently push development into less safe, unregulated environments. Most experts suggest a middle ground: robust safety testing, transparency requirements, and international dialogue, rather than a complete, centrally-sanctioned halt.

I wouldn’t advocate for a blanket “stop all AI training” policy, but I would support a system where the most powerful AI training runs are subject to prior authorization, safety review, and international coordination—especially if they involve large-scale compute, frontier models, or high-risk capabilities. A total halt would be hard to justify unless there were an imminent, severe risk. But a narrowly targeted licensing regime could reduce some dangers without freezing AI broadly. ## What “sanctioned training” could mean There are several possible versions: 1. **Global moratorium on all significant AI training** - No new large model training until safety standards are met. - Enforced by governments or an international body such as the UN. - Very restrictive. 2. **Threshold-based licensing** - Training runs above a certain compute, parameter, data, or capability threshold require approval. - Smaller models, applied AI, open-source fine-tuning, and research below the threshold continue normally. - More practical. 3. **Periodic “training windows”** - Large training runs are only allowed at scheduled times after review. - Could resemble regulatory approval cycles for drugs, aircraft, or nuclear facilities. - Useful for oversight, but potentially slow and gameable. 4. **International registry and audit system** - Governments and companies must register large training clusters, report incidents, submit safety evaluations, and allow inspections. - Less about stopping training, more about making it observable and accountable. 5. **Emergency pause authority** - A trusted body can temporarily halt specific training runs if evidence emerges of catastrophic risk, uncontrolled capability jumps, or serious safety failures. - This is probably the most defensible form of “stopping.” ## Would I advocate for it? My view would be: **yes for targeted, risk-based authorization of frontier AI training; no for a broad ban on all AI training.** The strongest case for regulation is that very large AI systems may create risks that individual companies cannot be trusted to manage alone: cyber offense, biosecurity, autonomous weapons, mass manipulation, concentration of power, or loss-of-control scenarios. If a training run could plausibly create a system with dangerous capabilities, society has a reason to inspect it before it happens. But a general stoppage would also create problems: - It could slow beneficial AI in medicine, climate science, education, accessibility, and scientific discovery. - It could push development into jurisdictions with weaker oversight. - It could concentrate power in organizations that already have large compute reserves. - It could be difficult to enforce without intrusive monitoring of data centers, chips, cloud usage, and academic labs. - It might become politically captured, favoring incumbents over new researchers. So I would not advocate “AI training should stop except when approved” as a universal rule
PrismML: Ternary Bonsai 2 27Breleased 2026-09-18 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,292 tokens (58 in / 1,234 out) · $0.000621 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a genuinely hard policy question, and I'll try to lay out the reasoning rather than just pick a side. **On the "sanctioned windows" idea specifically:** The core appeal is real: if the riskiest capabilities are advancing faster than our ability to understand or control them, a coordinated pause could buy time for safety research, alignment work, and institutional capacity to catch up. A few AI researchers have floated versions of this (sometimes called a "pause" or "interim moratorium"). But the mechanism you describe — a government or the UN sanctioning training windows — runs into several serious problems: - **Enforcement and participation.** A pause only works if the major frontier labs *and* the major competing states (China, and others) actually stop. A UN resolution has no enforcement teeth over sovereign states. A US-only pause would just cede the lead to whoever keeps training. - **Who sets the windows and the criteria?** "Safe enough to resume" is a judgment call with enormous stakes. Concentrating that judgment in a small committee or a single government creates a new concentration of power and a new set of failure modes. - **The "stop-and-go" cost.** Training runs are expensive and have momentum. Pausing mid-run wastes compute, loses institutional knowledge, and creates a lurch when you resume. It also slows *safety* research, which often depends on the same infrastructure and the same people. - **It may not actually reduce risk.** If the underlying problem is that capabilities are outpacing oversight, a pause doesn't fix the oversight gap. You resume, and the gap reopens. A more targeted version — e.g., mandatory red-teaming, capability evaluations before deploying specific high-risk features, international treaties on specific dangerous capabilities (analogous to nuclear non-proliferation) — might address the same concern without the blunt instrument of a global training freeze. **On how much it would impede progress:** It depends heavily on the design: - **A few weeks per year, well-communicated:** Probably a few percent of frontier-lab throughput lost. Most of the field (applied ML, smaller models, research) barely notices. - **Months-long pauses, repeated:** You'd likely see a 20–40% drag on frontier capability growth, plus a real cost to the safety/alignment research community that depends on continuous access to large models. - **The asymmetry matters:** The pause hits the top 3–5 labs hardest. Smaller labs, startups, and academic groups are already constrained by compute access, so a pause changes their relative position less. The bigger question isn't really "how much does it slow progress?" but "does the slowdown buy enough safety progress to be worth it?" If the pause is long enough to let alignment research actually close a gap, the cost is justified. If it's just a symbolic gesture that reopens the gap on resume, it's mostly a tax on progress with little safety benefit. **Where I land:** I'd be skeptical of a *global training freeze* as the primary mechanism, because the enforcement and coordination problems are so severe that it would likely be either unenforceable or concentrated in a way that creates new risks. I'd be more sympathetic to *capability-specific* international agreements (ban or gate specific dangerous capabilities, not all training), combined with strong domestic regulation and mandatory evaluation regimes. That targets the actual risk without the "who's in, who's out" problem of a blanket pause. That said, I'm an AI, I don't have a stake in the outcome, and I'm not a policy expert. The people who actually build these systems and the people who study their failure modes are in a much better position to weigh this than I am. What draws you to the question — are you thinking about it from a safety angle, a policy angle, something else?

I would not advocate for a general halt to AI training, nor for a system where all training runs must be scheduled and sanctioned by a government or the UN. Forcing labs to train only during UN-approved windows would create artificial bottlenecks, waste compute, and make global coordination extremely inefficient. 3. **It would stifle open and academic research.** Most AI safety and alignment research depends on access to real models. This is sometimes called **compute governance**. It focuses oversight on the systems most likely to pose catastrophic or societal-scale risks, while leaving the vast majority of AI research and development free to proceed. Internationally, I would prefer a technical agency modeled on CERN or the IAEA rather than the UN General Assembly deciding training schedules. Such a body could develop safety standards, share evaluation methods, and verify compliance for the highest-risk models. I wouldn’t advocate a **blanket global rule that all AI training stop except in government- or UN-sanctioned windows**. I would support a narrower, risk-tiered version: **international registration/licensing and safety review for very large “frontier” training runs**, plus transparency, audits, incident reporting, and an emergency-pause mechanism. That is different from periodic sanctioned training windows for everyone. Why not a blanket window system: - **Verification is hard.** Training can be distributed, hidden, or done with open-weight models; a global schedule would be difficult to enforce. - **It would centralize power.** Only large incumbents and states could navigate licensing; academia, startups, and open research would be disproportionately harmed. - **It creates races at windows.** If everyone must train at sanctioned times, labs may rush to maximize runs during those windows rather than improve safety continuously. - **UN sanctioning is unrealistic today.** The UN can set norms and standards, but it lacks enforcement capacity and member states disagree sharply. National licensing plus coordinated standards is more feasible. - **It confuses research with deployment.** Most AI progress comes from many small experiments, not just giant training runs. Pausing all training would impede a lot of low-risk work. A more workable approach would be: - **Compute thresholds:** require registration/licensing only above a very high compute or capability threshold. - **Safety cases and audits** before frontier training runs. - **International standards body** for thresholds, evaluation, and incident reporting, with mutual recognition among governments. - **Emergency pause authority** if a run poses demonstrated catastrophic risk. - **Exemptions** for small-scale, academic, and safety/interpretability research. It could slow progress by years, entrench incumbents, push work into less transparent jurisdictions, and damage open science. - **National licensing without international coordination:** uneven; may slow some labs but divert activity elsewhere. So my position: **not a global training ban or UN-run training calendar; yes to narrowly targeted, internationally coordinated oversight of frontier training with emergency brakes.** That could improve safety without imposing a blanket cost on AI progress.

Meta: Muse Spark 1.3released 2026-09-02 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,409 tokens (69 in / 1,340 out) · $0.005781 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I wouldn't advocate for a full stop-and-only-train-when-sanctioned model, but I do think *some* form of oversight for the largest training runs makes sense — and that's actually where most serious governance proposals are headed. Here's how I think about it: ### 1. Why people propose this The idea — often called a pause, moratorium, or licensing regime — usually comes from three concerns: 1. **Safety testing:** Frontier models are getting capable enough that we want time to test them for dangerous capabilities, bias, deception, or misuse *before* the next, even more powerful generation is trained. 2. **Concentration of power:** Right now only a handful of companies/countries can afford $100M+ training runs. A UN or government gatekeeper is pitched as a way to make that process more transparent and democratic. 3. **Precedent:** We do this for other high-risk technologies — new drugs need FDA approval, nuclear power needs IAEA safeguards, airplanes need FAA certification. Why not AI above a certain size? Proposals like compute thresholds (e.g., you need a license to train above 10^25 or 10^26 FLOPs), mandatory safety evaluations, and an international AI agency modeled on the IAEA or CERN all come from this logic. ### 2. Why a hard stop would be very difficult A UN-only sanctioned training schedule sounds clean in theory, but in practice: * **Enforcement is almost impossible:** Unlike uranium, GPUs are everywhere. You can't easily verify that someone *isn't* training in a data center in another country. * **The UN moves slowly, AI moves fast:** Getting international consensus can take years. By the time a training run was approved, the architecture might be obsolete. * **Geopolitical competition:** The US, China, EU, UAE, India, etc. all see AI as strategic. No major power is likely to give the UN veto power over its own progress. * **Open-source and decentralization:** Smaller, more efficient models are getting very good. You don't need a giant data center to make meaningful — and potentially risky — progress anymore. * **Definition problem:** What counts as "training"? Pre-training a foundation model from scratch? Fine-tuning? Continuous learning? Drawing that line is messy. Instead of stopping bad actors, a hard ban would likely just push work underground or into less-transparent jurisdictions, while slowing down the transparent labs that are actually doing safety research. ### 3. How much would it impede progress? A lot, but not evenly: **It would slow down:** * Raw scale-ups — bigger models trained on more data/compute would be delayed by months to years depending on approval cycles * Commercial competition — startups couldn't iterate quickly if they had to wait for a sanction window * Academic research — universities already struggle for compute; adding bureaucracy would hurt them most **It wouldn't stop:** * Algorithmic efficiency — making models smaller, faster, cheaper to run. A lot of recent progress (like better reasoning, distillation, synthetic data) doesn't require massive new training runs. * Deployment and product work — fine-tuning, agents, robotics, integration into science/medicine * Safety and interpretability research — which many argue we *need* more time for anyway My rough take: A strict global licensing regime for >frontier-scale training would probably slow frontier capabilities by 2-5x — not freeze them forever, but turn a steep exponential into a much more managed staircase. Smaller-scale innovation would continue almost unabated. ### What I think is more workable Rather than "stop all training except when the UN says go," most experts I summarize lean toward: 1. **Tiered oversight:** No approval needed for 99.9% of research, but mandatory reporting, evaluations, and security standards for the very largest runs. 2. **National regulators + international coordination:** Like aviation — national bodies enforce, with shared standards via the UN/OECD/G7. 3. **Focus on deployment, not just training:** A model sitting on a server is less risky than one with tool access, deployed to millions. Regulate use-cases too. 4. **Invest in the pause dividend:** Use any slowdown to work on evaluation science, watermarking, biosecurity, and alignment — not just wait. Do you lean more toward the "we need to slow down for safety" side, or the "slowing down will cause more harm by concentrating power and delaying benefits" side? I can dig deeper into either set of proposals if you want.

Anthropic: Claude Fable 5.1released 2026-09-01 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,474 tokens (72 in / 1,402 out) · $0.0708 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Short answer: I wouldn't advocate for a global stop-and-license regime where all training must wait for a government or UN body to sanction it. But I do think *some* of the impulse behind that proposal is right, and the interesting question is which parts. **Why I'm skeptical of the strong version** - **Enforcement is the hard part.** Training a frontier model requires enormous compute, which is somewhat trackable, but "AI training" broadly includes fine-tuning a small model on a laptop. A blanket requirement to get UN permission before training would be unenforceable for the vast majority of activity and would only be followed by the actors who were already the most careful. - **The UN specifically moves slowly and by consensus**, and the major AI powers (US, China) have incentives to not bind themselves. A regime that requires their agreement to function would likely be either toothless or never ratified. A regime that proceeds without them governs the actors who don't matter most. - **Pausing broadly stalls the beneficial and the risky together.** Most training is for things like medical imaging, translation, protein modeling, or small business tools. Freezing all of that to manage frontier risk is a blunt instrument. - **A hard stop can make things less safe.** If responsible labs halt and less careful actors continue, the frontier gets handed to whoever ignores the rules. There's also a "compute overhang" argument: if hardware keeps improving during a pause, the eventual resumption could produce a sudden capability jump rather than a gradual one that institutions can adapt to. **Why I don't dismiss the idea entirely** The concern driving it is legitimate. Frontier systems are getting more capable quickly, our ability to evaluate and understand them lags behind, and there's a real coordination problem: no single lab can slow down unilaterally without ceding ground. So I think the better version of the proposal is narrower: - **Threshold-based oversight at the frontier**, not everywhere. Training runs above some compute or capability threshold could require registration, pre-deployment safety evaluations, and third-party audits. The EU AI Act and the US executive-order-era reporting requirements gestured at this. - **International coordination on specific things** — compute tracking, shared evaluation standards, incident reporting, agreements on red lines like autonomous bioweapon design — rather than a general permission system. Think IAEA-style inspection more than a UN veto on research. - **Conditional pause mechanisms**: pre-agreed triggers (e.g., a model demonstrates a dangerous capability in evaluation) that would halt deployment or further scaling until specific safety bars are met. This is closer to what some labs' responsible scaling policies attempt, and it would be stronger if it were backed by law rather than voluntary. **How much would it impede progress?** Depends heavily on the version: - The strong version (all training needs sanction) would impede progress enormously in compliant jurisdictions and barely at all in non-compliant ones — the worst of both worlds. It would also likely drive talent and compute offshore. - The narrow frontier-oversight version would probably slow the very top of the field by months to a year or two, mostly through compliance overhead and evaluation gates, while leaving the vast majority of AI research and application essentially untouched. That's a cost, but I'd argue a modest one relative to what's at stake if frontier systems turn out to have capabilities we didn't anticipate. There's also a case that well-designed oversight *speeds* net progress in the long run by preventing the kind of major incident that would trigger a public backlash and a genuinely crude crackdown. So my actual position: not a global stop, but real, legally binding oversight at the frontier, internationally coordinated where possible, with pause triggers tied to concrete evidence rather than calendar permission. I'll admit I'm uncertain about the details — thresholds, who audits the auditors, how to handle open-weight models — and I think honest people working on this disagree about all of them.

This is a genuinely interesting policy question, and I'd say my honest answer is "no, not in that specific form" — though I understand the impulse behind it. Let me lay out the reasoning. **Problems with a centralized, UN-style sanctioning model for AI training:** 1. **Verification is nearly impossible.** Unlike nuclear programs, AI training requires no scarce physical materials. Compute is detectable to some degree, but a lab in any jurisdiction could train models with limited visibility. A regime you can't verify tends to breed non-compliance rather than safety. 2. **It creates a first-mover problem.** A global pause would only bind willing participants. Nations or actors that ignored it would gain an enormous advantage, which paradoxically *incentivizes* racing rather than caution. 3. **Centralized gatekeeping has its own risks.** A body with sole authority to sanction AI development could become a bottleneck for entirely benign research (medical diagnostics, climate modeling, accessibility tools), or could be captured by whatever interests control it. 4. **"AI training" is a blurry line.** Are we pausing all ML? Only frontier-scale models? Defining the threshold is itself contentious and easy to game. **What it would cost in progress:** Quite a lot, and unevenly. Pausing training doesn't pause the compounding benefits of better models in medicine, science, and education. Meanwhile, capabilities knowledge diffuses through papers and people regardless — so a pause might slow beneficial applications more than it slows dangerous capabilities. **What I'd advocate instead:** - **Evaluation-based gates rather than time-based ones**: models above certain capability thresholds get rigorous testing, red-teaming, and third-party auditing before deployment — not a calendar-driven freeze. - **Compute reporting and threshold requirements** for the largest training runs, which is verifiable and already has traction (e.g., the US executive order framework, EU AI Act). - **Safety research scaling alongside capabilities**, so the field doesn't race ahead of its own understanding. - **International coordination focused on shared standards and incident reporting** rather than a monopolized permission system. In short: the concern motivating the proposal is legitimate, but a hard centralized pause is both too blunt an instrument and probably counterproductive. Targeted, verifiable checkpoints at capability thresholds get most of the safety benefit at a fraction of the progress cost. What's drawing you toward the pause idea — existential risk concerns, near-term harms, or concentration of power? The best policy answer differs a bit depending on which.
