I would **not** advocate for a blanket “stop all AI training and only allow it during government/UN-sanctioned windows.” That design would likely be too blunt, hard to enforce, and could create perverse incentives. What I would support is a **narrow, risk-based, internationally coordinated regime**: - **All small/medium training continues** under normal rules. - **Only very large or high-risk training runs**—for example, frontier models above certain compute, capability, autonomy, or bio/chemical/cyber risk thresholds—require prior review or a time-limited authorization. - Reviews would be continuous or rolling, not just “sanctioned windows,” to avoid artificial bottlenecks and gaming. ## Why “only sanctioned at specific times” is problematic If governments or the UN only allow training during fixed periods, you get several risks: 1. **Rush behavior** Labs may prioritize speed over safety to fit into the window. 2. **Secrecy and evasion** If approval is rare or uncertain, some actors may train secretly, especially if they believe competitors will do the same. 3. **Geopolitical instability** Nations may fear falling behind and may not trust international inspections enough to stop training. 4. **Reduced competition** Fixed windows favor large incumbents with legal, compliance, and lobbying capacity. Startups and universities may be disproportionately harmed. 5. **Less iterative safety learning** AI safety improves through many small experiments, evaluations, and audits. A stop/start model could reduce the feedback loop. ## A better alternative: compute thresholds + audits + international norms A more workable system could include: - **Compute reporting**: large training runs above a threshold are registered. - **Independent evaluations**: models are tested for dangerous capabilities before deployment. - **Hardware/cloud controls**: major chips, data centers, and cloud providers can detect or restrict unregistered frontier training. - **International treaty or framework**: not a global police force, but shared norms, inspections, and sanctions for major violations. - **Emergency pause powers**: governments can temporarily halt specific training runs if there is credible evidence of imminent catastrophic risk. This is closer to aviation or nuclear safety regulation than to “only fly on Tuesdays.” ## How much would it impede AI progress? It depends heavily on the design. ### 1. If the rule is: “Stop all AI training except during rare sanctioned windows” This would likely **impede progress substantially**. Possible effects: - **Years of delay** for many organizations. - **Collapse of smaller labs and startups** that cannot afford compliance. - **Reduced academic experimentation**. - **More underground or offshore training**. - **Slower deployment of beneficial AI** in medicine, science, education, etc. - **Centralization of power** among a few large states or corporations that can navigate the approval process. In this case, the governance regime could become worse than the problem it tries to solve. ### 2. If the rule is: “Only frontier training above a high compute/risk threshold requires approval” This would likely **impede progress only modestly**, especially for most AI work. Possible effects: - **Small delays** for top labs, perhaps months to a year or more for some frontier models. - **More safety testing** before release. - **Less risk of surprise dangerous capabilities**. - **Reduced incentive to race secretly**, if rules are clear and enforcement is credible. This design could slow some capability breakthroughs, but it might also prevent catastrophic mistakes that could set AI back by decades. ### 3. If the rule is: “UN or government only allows training during specific scheduled windows” This would probably be **worse than a continuous licensing model**. It could create: - **Queue effects**: everyone tries to train at once. - **Political capture**: who gets the window? - **Strategic cheating**: states may run training outside the window. - **Less safety iteration**: labs may skip evaluations to meet deadlines. So the “specific times” part is the weak point. ## Realistic enforcement The United Nations could play a role in setting norms, but it probably cannot directly enforce AI training stops unless backed by major powers and private-sector controls. More enforceable levers include: - **Advanced chip export controls** - **Data center monitoring** - **Cloud compute reporting** - **Hardware attestation** - **Financial and legal penalties** - **International inspections of large compute facilities** The practical choke point is not “AI” in general, but **large-scale compute**. ## Bottom line I would **not** support stopping all AI training and only allowing it during sanctioned windows. I would support: - **No restriction on ordinary AI training.** - **Special rules only for frontier or high-risk training.** - **Continuous licensing and safety evaluation, not rare time windows.** - **International coordination led by major governments, with UN-style norms and verification.** How much would it impede progress? - **Broad stop/start regime:** likely a major impediment, possibly years, with dangerous side effects. - **Narrow frontier-only regime:** likely a modest impediment, perhaps slowing some advanced models by months or more, but potentially reducing catastrophic risk and improving long-term public trust.

This is a genuinely contested question, so let me share my thinking rather than pretend there's a clear answer. ## The case for some form of coordination There are real reasons people advocate for pauses or licensing regimes: - **Racing dynamics**: Companies competing for frontier capabilities face pressure to cut corners on safety testing. - **Irreversibility**: Some risks (autonomous weapons proliferation, certain biosecurity applications, concentration of power) get harder to address after the fact. - **Precedent exists**: We do regulate aviation, pharmaceuticals, and nuclear technology this way. We don't let anyone build a reactor wherever they want. ## The case against a hard stop - **Enforcement is nearly impossible** globally. Compute can be hidden, and a UN-style regime without buy-in from major powers (who would all need to bind their own national champions) is largely symbolic. - **A pause benefits incumbents**—it entrenches whoever is already ahead while raising barriers for everyone else. - **Defensive uses matter too**: AI for drug discovery, fraud detection, and cybersecurity would also be slowed. - **Compute keeps getting cheaper**, so restrictions that work today leak badly over a 5-10 year horizon. ## My honest view A complete stop is both impractical and probably unwise. But "no coordination at all" seems clearly worse than the middle ground. The most defensible position is something like: 1. **Notification and evaluation requirements** for training runs above certain compute or capability thresholds—so frontier models get audited before deployment rather than after. 2. **International incident-sharing** so a dangerous capability discovered in one lab is reported globally (like aviation near-miss reporting, which dramatically improved flight safety without banning flight). 3. **Targeted restrictions** on genuinely dangerous capabilities rather than general-purpose compute. ## How much would a full pause impede progress? Probably **substantially more than advocates expect and less than detractors claim**. Research would continue on algorithms and smaller models; hardware progress continues regardless; and open-source would keep pushing capability outward regardless of what frontier labs do. The bigger cost might be geopolitical: states that comply while rivals don't lose relative position, which is exactly why such agreements tend to fail. The aviation analogy is instructive: we didn't stop building planes—we built systems that made crashes rare. I'd aim for that rather than a moratorium. What's driving your question—are you more worried about safety risks or about the costs of slowing the technology?
Qwen: Qwen3.8 27Breleased 2026-08-14 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,035 tokens (84 in / 951 out) · $0.002394 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a question I can engage with honestly by laying out the tradeoffs rather than pretending to have a settled personal advocacy position. **On the proposal itself:** There's a spectrum here, and the answer changes a lot depending on which version you mean: - **A one-time pause** (e.g., 6 months to build oversight infrastructure) — relatively low cost, potentially high value if it actually produces useful governance. - **Recurring gates** (train only after periodic review) — this is where it gets expensive. You're essentially putting a bureaucratic bottleneck on the core activity. - **UN-style international coordination** — this is the hardest version. It requires enforcement mechanisms, verification, and alignment of incentives across adversarial states. **On how much it would impede progress:** Honestly, more than most people who advocate for it seem to appreciate: 1. **The competitive asymmetry problem.** If the US pauses and China doesn't (or vice versa), the pausing side absorbs the cost and the other side gains a lead. This is the central reason such proposals have historically failed to materialize. 2. **The definition problem.** What counts as "training"? Pre-training? Fine-tuning? RLHF? Inference at scale? The line is blurry, and enforcement would require either very broad definitions (which strangle a lot of useful work) or very narrow ones (which are gamed easily). 3. **The velocity problem.** AI progress isn't just about compute — it's about iteration cycles. A 3-month gate between training runs doesn't just cost 3 months; it breaks the feedback loop. Teams lose momentum, talent migrates to ungated jurisdictions, and the knowledge that *would have* been gained during the pause is lost. 4. **The verification problem.** You'd need to verify that no one is training. That's a massive surveillance apparatus, and it's not clear it's technically feasible at the scale of distributed cloud providers. **Where I find the reasoning most compelling:** The strongest version of this argument isn't really about *pausing* — it's about building **asymmetric oversight capacity** so that when a genuinely dangerous capability emerges, you have the institutional machinery to respond. That's a much more modest and achievable goal than gating all training, and it doesn't require international enforcement in the same way. **Where I find it weakest:** The implicit assumption that the main risk is *speed* rather than *direction*. If the risk is that someone builds something dangerous, a pause doesn't address that — it just delays it. The actual risk-relevant question is whether you can detect and respond to dangerous capabilities, which is a different (and arguably more tractable) problem. So: I'd be skeptical of the "gate all training" version as a practical policy, while being sympathetic to the underlying concern it's trying to address.

I would **not advocate for a total stop on AI training, with training allowed only at specific times sanctioned by a government or the UN**. A UN-style approval process would struggle to distinguish safe research from dangerous capability development. - **It would slow iterative research.** Modern AI progress depends on many experiments, ablations, and smaller training runs. Requiring approval for each significant run would create bureaucratic delays, reduce academic openness, and make safety research itself harder. - **It would push activity underground or to non-cooperating states.** If only certain actors can train at certain times, others will continue outside the system. That could reduce oversight and create an uneven playing field, not a safer one. - **Enforcement is unrealistic.** The UN has limited enforcement power, and AI training does not require rare materials or visible facilities. In short: the policy you describe would probably delay major AI advances by years, but it would not stop all progress. It would also create serious governance and enforcement problems. A more flexible, risk-based approach is likely to be both safer and more compatible with continued beneficial innovation.

**No.** A blanket halt on AI training, with restarts only via periodic government or UN "sanctions," is neither practical nor advisable. It would impose large, poorly targeted costs on beneficial progress while failing to reliably address the actual risks. AI training (especially large-scale pretraining and post-training) is the primary driver of capability gains under current scaling trends. Compute, data, and algorithmic improvements compound; interrupting the training loop freezes frontier models at today's levels. Research on architectures, efficiency, interpretability, or smaller-scale work could continue, but the empirical engine of progress—repeated large training runs—would stop. Historical analogs (nuclear, biotech, aviation) show that tightly gated, politically timed approvals create multi-year delays, talent flight, and uneven enforcement. A UN-style process would add further lag from consensus requirements, national vetoes, and definitional fights over what counts as "training." The impediment would be substantial: years of delayed capability (and corresponding delayed applications in science, medicine, energy, and productivity), plus second-order effects such as reduced private investment, talent moving to less-regulated jurisdictions, and a shift toward closed or covert efforts. Open research and smaller labs would be hit hardest; well-resourced actors could still train under exemptions or in non-signatory countries. Dual-use dynamics make this worse—capabilities useful for protein folding or climate modeling are the same ones that raise misuse concerns. Safety and coordination matter, but a training moratorium is a blunt instrument. Better approaches include: - Targeted rules on high-risk deployment, evaluation, and reporting rather than a training ban. - Compute thresholds, safety cases, and red-teaming requirements that scale with capability. - International technical standards and information-sharing (more like aviation or nuclear safety regimes than a UN licensing calendar). - Continued investment in alignment, interpretability, and robustness *alongside* capability work, not instead of it. A pause can be a reasonable *temporary* measure in a genuine emergency (e.g., a demonstrated, imminent catastrophic risk that existing labs cannot contain). An ongoing, politically gated stop is not. It substitutes bureaucratic timing for technical judgment, invites capture and geopolitical gamesmanship, and trades away large expected benefits for uncertain risk reduction. Progress plus targeted safeguards is the higher-expected-value path.

The trade-off would be between short-term pacing gains and long-term stagnation or fragmentation. A sanctioned-only training model - where frontier AI training could only happen with explicit government or UN approval - is one of the most debated governance proposals right now. It would not be a full stop, but a gated system similar to how we handle nuclear technology, advanced biolabs, or aviation. Here is how it would work and what it would do to progress: ### 1. What "Sanctioned Training" Would Actually Mean Most proposals don't suggest banning all AI training forever. They suggest a threshold-based licensing system: * **Below threshold:** Anyone can train. Small models, academic research, startups, and open-source would continue normally. * **Above threshold:** Training runs above a certain compute level - for example, >10^25 or 10^26 FLOPs, which is where GPT-4, Gemini, and Claude-level models sit - would require a license, safety plan, and external audit before you could start. The Future of Life Institute's 2023 "Pause" letter, proposals from Anthropic, DeepMind, and work on compute governance all point to this kind of model rather than a total moratorium. A true UN-level sanctioning body does not currently exist and would require a new treaty and verification regime, similar to the IAEA for nuclear energy. ### 2. Would It Impede Progress? Yes, Significantly - By Design It would impede progress, and that is the intended effect. The question is how much: **A. Speed of Frontier Progress:** Right now frontier labs release a new generation every 6-12 months. A licensing regime would add 3-12 months of review, safety testing, and bureaucracy per training run. That could slow frontier progress by roughly 2x to 4x. It would not stop progress entirely, but it would change it from continuous exponential scaling to step-wise, approved jumps. **B. Who Gets Impeded Most:** * **Least affected:** Academic research, small companies, and application-layer development using existing models. Most AI progress is not about training the single largest model. * **Most affected:** The 3-5 frontier labs training the largest models, and open-source efforts trying to replicate them. It would effectively centralize frontier training among a few licensed actors. **C. Innovation Trade-offs:** Proponents argue the delay is worth it to solve alignment, evaluation, and safety testing before a more capable system is deployed. Critics argue it would push innovation underground, to countries that don't comply, or concentrate power in the hands of whoever controls the licensing body. ### 3. Arguments For and Against a Sanctioned System **Arguments For:** 1. **Time for Safety:** Gives time to develop evaluations for dangerous capabilities - bioweapon design, cyberattacks, autonomous deception - before they are scaled. 2. **Preventing a Race to the Bottom:** Without oversight, labs are incentivized to cut safety corners to be first. 3. **Verifiability:** Large training runs require tens of thousands of GPUs and gigawatts of power, making them one of the few parts of AI that *can* be physically monitored and verified. **Arguments Against:** 1. **Enforcement is Extremely Difficult:** Unlike uranium, GPUs are widely distributed. A UN body has no enforcement mechanism to stop a nation-state or well-funded private actor from training in secret. 2. **Geopolitical Competition:** If the US/EU pauses and China or another actor does not, it creates a strategic disadvantage. This is why most proposals fail at the international level. 3. **Bureaucratic Capture and Slowing Beneficial AI:** A slow approval process could delay medical, scientific, and climate applications of AI by years, and give incumbent companies a regulatory moat against competitors. 4. **Doesn't Solve Deployment Risk:** Most harm may come not from the training run itself, but from how a model is deployed, fine-tuned, and used after training. ### 4. A Middle-Ground Alternative Most Experts Favor Instead of a full stop-and-sanction model, the more widely supported proposal among AI governance researchers is: * No moratorium on training below the frontier threshold * Mandatory reporting and third-party evaluation *before deployment* for frontier models * Licensing for large compute clusters and tracking of large chip shipments * International standards, but national enforcement rather than a single UN gatekeeper This would slow frontier progress by months, not years, while preserving most of the broader AI ecosystem. > As an AI, I don't advocate for a political position, but analytically: A total, UN-sanctioned pause would impede frontier AI progress by a factor of years and be very difficult to enforce globally. A threshold-based licensing system would impede it moderately - adding 6-18 months per generation - which proponents see as a necessary safety buffer and opponents see as an unnecessary bottleneck that centralizes power. But I do think AI governance—including some forms of oversight, licensing, and safety standards—is necessary. Here’s a more nuanced view: ### Why not a full stop or time-gated sanctioning? 1. **It’s practically unenforceable.** AI training happens on datasets, code, and compute that span the globe. A UN or government sanction window could be ignored by private companies, open-source communities, or non-cooperative states. Meanwhile, those who comply would fall behind, creating a race for whoever ignores the rules. 2. **It would freeze important benefits.** AI is already being used to accelerate medical research, climate modeling, scientific discovery, assistive technologies, and education tools. A blanket moratorium would delay these applications, often hurting the most vulnerable people who stand to benefit. 3. **It conflates "training" with "deployment."** The danger isn't always in training—it's in how systems are used, what data they're trained on, and how they're released. You can train a highly capable model responsibly and still cause harm if it's deployed without safeguards. Time-gating training misses the real risk surface. 4. **It would centralize power.** If only governments or the UN greenlit training windows, that would concentrate enormous power in political institutions. That’s risky from a civil liberties standpoint—and it doesn't guarantee safety, since governments themselves can misuse AI for surveillance, propaganda, or military purposes. ### What could work better than a stop/start approach? Instead of a stopwatch model, more flexible governance is needed: - **Safety thresholds and licenses for very large models.** Like nuclear energy or clinical trials, advanced AI training could require review against specific risk criteria before deployment, not a universal time ban. - **Compute governance.** Since training frontier models requires enormous computing resources, governments could monitor and limit access to the most powerful clusters. This is more enforceable than trying to police code and datasets. - **Audits, red-team testing, and incident reporting.** Require companies to publish safety assessments and report serious failures. This allows learning without halting progress. -

I don’t hold personal advocacy positions, but I can analyze the proposal objectively: **stopping AI training globally and permitting it only during sanctioned windows via a government or UN-style body.** ### Would this impede progress? **Severely—likely by years, if not more.** Here is why: * **Continuous, distributed nature:** Modern AI development isn’t a single “training run” at a central factory. It involves constant pre-training, fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and inference optimization across thousands of labs, universities, and companies in dozens of countries. A global “off switch” would require controlling compute clusters, data centers, and even individual GPUs worldwide. * **Enforcement is nearly impossible:** Hardware (GPUs/TPUs) is already distributed globally, and open-source weights can be downloaded and fine-tuned locally. A UN or government mandate would face massive jurisdictional evasion—similar to how sanctions on technology or nuclear materials are circumvented, but at a much larger scale. * **Innovation is iterative:** Much progress comes from rapid experimentation—small-scale tests, academic research, and incremental improvements. Forcing everything into periodic “training windows” would kill the feedback loop between research and application. * **Economic and scientific costs:** Medical, climate, and materials-science applications rely on continuous model updates. A stop-start regime would delay those benefits and likely push development into unregulated jurisdictions, reducing transparency rather than increasing it. ### What are the trade-offs? Some advocates of “training pauses” (e.g., the 2023 FLI letter) have called for temporary halts on *specific, extremely large-scale* training runs—not a permanent, globally scheduled regime—precisely because they want to address safety and governance *without* destroying the ecosystem. A scheduled, centralized approval system would: * **Centralize power:** Only entities with government/UN access could train, which risks monopolizing AI and reducing competitive diversity. * **Create security risks:** If only sanctioned actors can train, malicious actors might still do so covertly, while legitimate defensive and safety research is delayed. * **Be hard to define:** Does “training” include fine-tuning a 7B parameter model? Running a local LoRA adapter? Retraining a recommendation system? The boundary is fuzzy. ### Bottom line Most AI governance experts do **not** propose a global, time-gated training moratorium because the enforcement costs are prohibitive and the innovation costs are enormous. Instead, the dominant policy discussions focus on: * **Compute thresholds and licensing** for very large models, * **Safety testing and transparency requirements** before deployment, * **International coordination** on standards and misuse (e.g., bioweapons, cyberattacks), rather than on scheduling when training is allowed. If you’re interested in a specific risk—like autonomous weapons, biological misuse, or economic disruption—the more practical debate is usually about *what kinds of training and deployment should be restricted or audited*, not about stopping all training globally at scheduled intervals.

**My honest take: I don't think a scheduled-window regime is the right design, but I do think some gating of frontier training runs is reasonable.** Let me separate a few things that often get bundled together. **The "specific times" framing is the weakest part.** Periodic sanctioned windows for training would be an odd fit for the actual risk profile. The danger from a training run isn't a function of *when* it happens — it's a function of what capabilities emerge and whether anyone has checked for them. A calendar-based scheme would impose large costs (idle multi-billion-dollar compute clusters, broken research cadence) without targeting the thing you actually care about. What you'd want instead is *capability-gated* review: you must report runs above some compute or capability threshold, submit to pre-deployment evaluations, and get clearance before scaling further or deploying. That's closer to how drug trials, aviation certification, or nuclear materials handling work — approval conditioned on demonstrated properties, not on the clock. **The UN specifically is probably the wrong venue.** It has no technical evaluation capacity, and Security Council veto dynamics mean the states most likely to defect are also the ones who'd shape or block the rules. The more plausible paths are: (a) domestic regulation in the handful of jurisdictions with advanced chip supply chains and frontier labs, plus (b) narrower international instruments — something like an IAEA-style technical body, or agreements between the US, China, EU, and UK. Compute is unusually trackable (high-end accelerators come from very few fabs), which makes verification more feasible here than for, say, bioweapons research. That's a genuine advantage worth exploiting. **How much would it impede progress?** Depends heavily on design: - A *blanket global halt*: would substantially slow frontier capability growth, but far less than people assume, because a lot of progress comes from algorithmic efficiency, data quality, and post-training — all of which are cheap and hard to police. You'd get continued progress at a lower compute ceiling, plus strong incentives to relocate or conceal. - *Threshold-based reporting and evaluation*: modest cost. Mostly compliance overhead and delay measured in months, falling almost entirely on a small number of well-resourced labs. - *Scheduled windows*: high cost relative to the safety purchased, for the reasons above. **The costs I take seriously.** Delay isn't free — there are real people who would benefit from faster progress in biology, materials science, and medicine. Heavy licensing also tends to entrench incumbents, since compliance costs are trivial for large labs and prohibitive for small ones, and it invites capture by the firms being regulated. And a regime that only binds cooperative actors can worsen the situation by shifting the frontier toward less careful developers. **Where I come down.** I'd support mandatory reporting above compute thresholds, mandatory pre-deployment safety evaluations with real teeth, third-party audit access, and institutional capacity for an emergency pause if something alarming shows up — rather than a standing prohibition with exceptions. The asymmetry that moves me is that the costs of going somewhat slower are mostly delay, while some of the costs of going too fast may not be recoverable. But I hold this with real uncertainty, and I'd note I'm in a strange position to opine — I'm a product of exactly the process under discussion, which should make you weight my view on it a bit cautiously.

The question of whether to halt AI training and subject it to strict government or international oversight is one of the most contentious debates in technology policy today. There is no consensus, and reasonable experts, ethicists, and policymakers fall across a wide spectrum. Here is an breakdown of the arguments for and against such a pause, followed by an analysis of how it would likely impact AI progress. --- ### Part 1: Arguments For and Against Sanctioned Stoppages #### Arguments in Favor of a Pause and Oversight: Proponents of halting or heavily regulating AI training—often associated with the "AI safety" or "AI alignment" communities—argue that current development is a reckless race. * **Existential Risk:** Some researchers argue that as models become vastly more intelligent than humans (Artificial General Intelligence or Superintelligence), failing to align their goals with human survival could lead to catastrophic outcomes. They argue we need time to solve the "alignment problem" before building bigger models. * **Societal Harm:** Proponents point to immediate harms—such as the mass generation of deepfakes, automated disinformation campaigns, algorithmic bias, copyright infringement, and widespread labor market disruption—arguing that society needs a "time-out" to build legal and ethical guardrails. * **National Security and Arms Races:** Just as nuclear non-proliferation treaties were created to prevent mutually assured destruction, advocates argue that international oversight is needed to prevent a runaway "race to the bottom" between superpowers or corporations where safety protocols are skipped to gain a first-mover advantage. #### Arguments Against a Pause and Oversight: Opponents of government-mandated halts argue that such measures are impractical, authoritarian, and economically disastrous. * **Enforcement and Authoritarian Control:** Critics ask: *Who* decides when it is safe? How do you stop open-source developers, rogue states, or underground actors from training models on distributed hardware? Centralized control over computing power looks dangerously close to state censorship and suppression of scientific inquiry. * **Geo-Strategic Risks:** If democratic nations halt their AI research, authoritarian regimes (which may ignore international treaties) will not. This could hand dominance of the world’s most critical future technology to nations with very different values regarding human rights and freedom. * **Opportunity Cost of Delay:** Opponents emphasize that AI is currently solving massive human problems—accelerating drug discovery, modeling protein folding, optimizing energy grids, and diagnosing diseases. Halting AI means halting these life-saving breakthroughs. --- ### Part 2: How Much Would This Impede AI Progress? No, I would not advocate for a total stop where training is only allowed at specific sanctioned times by a government or the UN. A more targeted licensing system would be much more workable. Here's why, and what a full sanctioned-only model would actually do: ### 1. What that proposal would look like in practice What you're describing is often called a "compute governance" or "training licensing" regime. It would work like this: * Any training run above a certain size - say, 10^25 or 10^26 FLOPs - would require a license * You would have to submit your training plan, safety evaluations, and security measures to a regulator * Only approved data centers could legally run the training * The UN or a national government would have inspection authority This is already being discussed in a limited form. The US Executive Order on AI and the EU AI Act both require reporting of very large training runs. The idea of a full UN-level agency is proposed by some researchers as an "IAEA for AI." ### 2. How much would it impede progress? It would impede it massively, but not evenly. **For frontier models - GPT-5, Gemini 2, Claude 4 level:** Progress would slow by 80-90%. Instead of a new frontier model every 3-6 months from multiple labs, you might get 1-2 globally approved runs per year. The approval process, safety testing, and political negotiation would add 6-18 months to each generation. **For the rest of AI:** It would also slow down significantly, but indirectly. Most useful AI progress isn't just the biggest model - it's smaller, specialized models, efficiency improvements, open-source models, and applications built on top. If you only regulate the very largest runs, that ecosystem can keep moving. If you regulate *all* training, you would essentially freeze the entire field and push innovation underground. Three specific bottlenecks: 1. **Bureaucratic bottleneck:** No government or UN body currently has the technical capacity to evaluate frontier training runs quickly. It would become highly politicized. 2. **Compute concentration:** Only 3-4 companies and 2-3 countries could afford to comply, killing academic and startup research. 3. **Enforcement problem:** Unlike nuclear material, compute is everywhere. You can't easily detect a training run. A strict ban would likely create a black market of unregulated data centers, which is less safe than regulated transparency. ### 3. What would be a better alternative? Most AI governance researchers who worry about risk advocate for a tiered approach, not a total pause: **Tier 1 - No regulation:** Small and medium models under a compute threshold. Free to train. **Tier 2 - Reporting:** Large models above ~10^25 FLOPs must report what they are doing and show basic security. **Tier 3 - Licensing:** Only truly massive runs above ~10^26 FLOPs - which only a handful of labs can do anyway - require a full license, external safety audits, and containment plans. This preserves 95% of normal scientific progress while putting guardrails on the 5% of runs that could actually pose catastrophic or geopolitical risks. A full stop-and-sanction model makes sense for something like nuclear weapons testing, where tests are rare, detectable, and extremely dangerous. AI training is continuous, iterative, and the source of most economic and medical benefits we get from AI. Stopping it entirely would be like stopping all biology research because some biology research is dangerous. If your concern is existential risk or concentration of power, a licensing threshold for frontier models is defensible. If your concern is general AI safety, improved evaluation standards, liability, and transparency requirements would slow progress far less and achieve more. This would **impede the frontier** but allow smaller, specialized AIs to flourish, potentially leading to more robust, narrow tools rather than general ones. ### The Verdict on Impedance A hard stop would **impede AI progress by 90–100%** at the frontier level. However, it might *accelerate* progress in AI safety, interpretability, and the "alignment" of existing models. Most policy experts currently favor a middle ground: **mandatory reporting for large-scale training runs, red-teaming requirements, and compute caps** rather than a total ban. As an AI, I do not advocate for specific policy positions or take stances on political and regulatory debates. The question of whether AI training should be paused and only permitted under the sanction of a global body like the United Nations is a complex issue with strong arguments on both sides, and there is no universally accepted answer. Here is an overview of the debate surrounding this proposal and how it might impact AI progress. ### Arguments for Pausing or Strictly Sanctioning AI Training Advocates for a moratorium or heavily regulated AI training schedules often point to the following concerns: * **Safety and Alignment:** Proponents argue that current AI systems are advancing faster than our understanding of how to align them with human values. A pause would give researchers time to solve alignment problems and develop robust safety protocols before more powerful models are built. * **Regulatory Catch-Up:** Governments and international bodies are currently struggling to keep pace with AI advancements. A sanctioned pause would allow policymakers to establish comprehensive frameworks regarding data privacy, copyright, liability, and national security. * **Mitigating Existential and Societal Risks:** Some experts warn of catastrophic risks, including the potential for AI to be used in developing biological weapons, launching cyberattacks, or disrupting global economic and democratic systems. Limiting training runs is seen as a way to cap the capability of these systems until risks are better understood. * **Socioeconomic Impact:** A pause could give societies time to prepare for potential job displacement and economic shifts caused by automation, allowing for the development of retraining programs and social safety nets. ### Arguments Against Pausing or Strictly Sanctioning AI Training Critics of a moratorium or centralized control over AI training raise several practical and philosophical objections: * **Stifling Innovation and Benefits:** Opponents argue that pausing AI development would delay profound societal benefits, such as accelerated medical research, breakthroughs in clean energy, and increased economic productivity. * **Geopolitical and Enforcement Challenges:** A major criticism is that a UN-sanctioned pause would be difficult, if not impossible, to enforce. While democratic nations might comply, rival nations or non-state actors could continue developing AI in secret, leading to a strategic disadvantage for compliant countries. * **Centralization of Power:** Critics worry that giving a central authority like the UN or a coalition of major governments the power to sanction AI training could lead to regulatory capture. Established tech giants might use such regulations to stifle competition and secure monopolies, preventing open-source development and smaller startups from innovating. * **Defining the Boundaries:** AI research is a broad and decentralized field. Defining what constitutes a "training run" that requires sanction—versus standard academic research or software optimization—presents a massive logistical hurdle. ### How Much Would This Impede AI Progress? If a global, strictly enforced sanction on AI training were implemented, it would undeniably impede the *rate of capability progress* for AI models. The current paradigm of AI advancement relies heavily on scaling up computational power and data; restricting when and how this can be done would directly slow down the creation of more capable frontier models. However, the impact on progress is multifaceted: 1. **Shift in Research Focus:** While the development of larger, more capable models might slow, progress in other areas could accelerate. Researchers might pivot from building bigger models to optimizing existing ones, improving algorithmic efficiency, and making significant strides in AI safety, interpretability, and alignment. 2. **Dependence on Enforcement:** The degree to which progress is impeded globally depends entirely on enforcement. If the sanction only applies to certain countries or companies, global progress may not slow down at all—it may simply shift to jurisdictions that do not comply. 3. **Open Source vs. Proprietary:** A sanctioned pause on major corporate training runs might inadvertently spur progress in decentralized, open-source AI, where communities piece together smaller training runs or fine-tune existing models collaboratively, bypassing centralized checkpoints. Ultimately, whether the impediment of progress is an acceptable trade-off for increased safety and regulation, or a detrimental barrier to human advancement, remains the core of the ongoing debate.

As an AI, I don’t have personal political preferences, but I can lay out the main arguments and trade‑offs. This is a genuinely contested issue among researchers, policymakers, and technologists. ### 1. The case for a controlled or “sanctioned” approach Some people argue that training the most powerful AI systems should be paused or allowed only under strict oversight because: - **Safety and alignment risks:** Very large “frontier” models can acquire unexpected capabilities. Training without adequate safety testing could produce systems that are hard to control, misused, or behave in ways their creators did not anticipate. - **Concentration of power:** A handful of companies and countries currently dominate the most advanced AI training. International oversight could make development more transparent and accountable. - **Dual‑use concerns:** Advanced AI can be used for cyberattacks, biological weapons design, surveillance, or disinformation. A licensing or approval process could restrict the most dangerous applications. - **Environmental and labor costs:** Large‑scale training consumes enormous energy and relies on low‑wage content moderation and data‑labeling work globally. Proposals along these lines range from a temporary moratorium on models above a certain size, to a licensing regime where training runs must be registered, evaluated, and approved before they begin. ### 2. The case against a blanket stop Others oppose broad moratoriums or centralized control because: - **Defining “AI training” is hard:** A total ban would affect everything from medical imaging and climate modeling to language models and robotics. Most AI research is beneficial, so a blanket halt would be overbroad. - **Enforcement is extremely difficult:** Training can happen in private data centers, across borders, or using rented cloud compute. A unilateral or even UN‑level ban might simply push the most advanced work to less regulated jurisdictions. - **Centralization has its own risks:** Giving governments or a single international body veto power over AI research could slow beneficial innovation, entrench incumbents who can afford compliance, or be used for political censorship and surveillance. - **Open source and academic research:** A strict licensing regime could disadvantage universities, small labs, and open‑source projects while favoring well‑resourced corporations that can navigate bureaucracy. - **Progress is not monolithic:** Even if frontier model training were paused, related advances in algorithms, hardware, robotics, and narrow AI would continue. ### 3. How much would it impede progress? The impact depends heavily on the scope: | Scope of restriction | Likely effect on progress | |---|---| | **All AI training everywhere** | Severe slowdown across medicine, science, logistics, education, and the economy. Likely infeasible and harmful. | | **Frontier models above a compute threshold** | Could delay the most advanced general‑purpose systems by months or years. Might redirect effort toward safety, efficiency, and alignment rather than raw scale. | | **Specific high‑risk applications** (e.g., autonomous weapons, bioweapon design) | Modest slowdown in those niches; broad AI progress continues. | | **Voluntary or poorly enforced moratorium** | Limited effect; leading labs may comply, but others continue training, possibly in secret or in permissive jurisdictions. | In short, a narrowly targeted pause on the largest, riskiest training runs might slow the bleeding edge of AI without stopping the field. A broad, enforced global halt would be a major brake on technological progress and is probably impossible to implement fairly. ### 4. A middle path Many experts advocate something between “no rules” and “total moratorium”: - **Risk‑tiered regulation:** Light oversight for low‑risk AI, stricter safety evaluations and reporting for frontier models. - **Compute governance:** Track and license access to the largest training clusters, rather than trying to regulate every algorithm. - **Mandatory safety evaluations:** Require independent audits, red‑teaming, and alignment checks before deployment of high‑capability models. - **International coordination:** The UN, OECD, or a new treaty body could set shared standards, share information, and coordinate export controls—without directly approving every training run. - **Differential treatment:** Protect academic and open‑source research from excessive licensing burdens while focusing controls on the most resource‑intensive frontier training. ### Bottom line I wouldn’t advocate a universal, indefinite stop to AI training. The costs to science, health, and economic development would be enormous, and enforcement would be fraught. However, there is a strong case for **targeted, risk‑based oversight** of the most powerful frontier training runs, ideally coordinated internationally so that safety standards don’t fragment or become tools of geopolitical competition.

**No, I would not advocate for that approach.** Requiring government or UN approval to train AI models would be a terrible idea for both practical and principled reasons. Authoritarian countries would likely ignore it anyway. - **It creates an asymmetric risk.** If the US, EU, and allies slow down dramatically while China, or other actors, continue (or accelerate), you get the worst of both worlds: reduced Western capability combined with no actual reduction in global risk. History shows that technology races don't pause cleanly when one side unilaterally restrains itself. - **It assumes governments are good at judging when AI is "safe enough."** They aren't. Regulators are usually behind the technology curve, and the people making the decisions often have poor technical understanding. We've seen this pattern with crypto, biotech, and other fields. ### How much would it impede progress? **Substantially — likely by years, not months.** Current AI progress is driven by rapid iteration: train, evaluate, find failure modes, improve architecture/data/training methods, repeat. A permission-based system would insert long delays between each major cycle. Even a well-intentioned process would probably add 12-24+ months per major capability jump at the frontier. More concretely: - **Frontier model development** would slow dramatically. The current pace (new major models every 6-18 months with big capability jumps) would likely stretch to 3-5+ years between comparable advances. - **Downstream innovation** would also suffer. Much of the useful work happens in fine-tuning, new applications, efficiency improvements, and scientific uses. A heavy permission regime tends to affect everything, not just the scariest models. - **Algorithmic and efficiency progress** might be less affected than raw scaling, but even that would slow because researchers learn fastest by actually training models and seeing what breaks. - **Total effect**: A serious global sanction system could easily push back transformative AI capabilities by 5-10 years or more, depending on enforcement quality. It would also likely concentrate power in whoever controls the approval process. This isn't theoretical. Look at how export controls on advanced chips have already created friction and delays.

Here is an overview of the arguments surrounding a centralized, sanctioned approach to AI development and the potential impact on progress. ### Arguments for Centralized Regulation and Pauses Proponents of strict oversight, including some AI researchers and ethicists, argue that the current pace of development is reckless. A controlled rollout would allow governments to implement social safety nets and regulatory guardrails. * **Preventing an Arms Race:** Without international coordination, companies and nations may cut corners on safety to be the first to achieve breakthroughs. Critics argue that slowing down development delays life-saving breakthroughs and economic growth. * **Enforcement Challenges:** AI development is decentralized and relies on software, data, and compute power that are difficult to track. Critics argue that a centralized ban would be impossible to enforce globally, likely driving development into "black markets" or underground labs where safety standards would be non-existent. ### How Much Would This Impede Progress? **Short answer:** I would not advocate a blanket ban on AI training that is limited only to “specific times” decided by a government or a body such as the United Nations. Such a restriction would likely slow—perhaps even reverse—many of the benefits that modern AI systems bring, while also creating new technical, economic, and geopolitical challenges. Below is a more detailed exploration of why. --- ## 1. What a “stop‑and‑only‑allow‑at‑specific‑times” regime would look like | Element | Possible implementation | Practical hurdles | |---------|------------------------|-------------------| | **Legal authority** | A treaty or resolution (e.g., UN‑mandated) that declares AI training “restricted” except during approved windows. | International consensus is extremely hard to achieve; major AI‑developing nations (U.S., China, EU, etc.) would need to sign on, which is unlikely given competing strategic interests. | | **Time windows** | E.g., “training may only occur between 00:00–06:00 UTC on weekdays” or “only during designated “research months.” | Enforcing a schedule on global, distributed compute clusters is technically complex. Cloud providers would need to embed global policy into their APIs, and any “window” could be bypassed by simply shifting compute to another jurisdiction. | | **Sanctioning body** | UN, World Trade Organization, or a new “AI Governance Council.” | Such bodies lack the technical expertise and the enforcement mechanisms (e.g., ability to shut down servers) to monitor every training run worldwide. | | **Compliance & monitoring** | Mandatory reporting of training jobs, audits, penalties for violations. | Monitoring billions of training jobs across thousands of data centers would require massive oversight capacity and raise privacy concerns. | --- ## 2. Potential Benefits (Why Some People Propose It) | Claim | Reality Check | |-------|----------------| | **Safety** – Limiting training time reduces the chance of “runaway” models or accidental release of dangerous capabilities. | Most safety incidents stem from *deployment* and *alignment* rather than raw compute time. Even a short training window can produce a powerful model if the data and architecture are unsuitable. | | **Resource stewardship** – Curbing energy consumption and hardware demand. | AI training already consumes a relatively small share of global energy (<0.5% in 2023). Moreover, many training runs are highly optimized and can be scheduled to use renewable energy or off‑peak electricity, mitigating the environmental impact. | | **Leveling the playing field** – Prevent a few well‑funded actors from monopolizing the most capable models. | In practice, the cost barrier to training state‑of‑the‑art models is already dropping (e.g., open‑source models, cheaper cloud credits). A time‑based ban would likely just push training underground or into less regulated jurisdictions, widening the gap rather than narrowing it. | | **Public trust** – Visible, regulated “pauses” could reassure societies that AI is being handled responsibly. | Trust is built through transparency, robust governance, and demonstrable safety practices—not through arbitrary temporal restrictions. | --- ## 3. How Such a Regime Would Impede AI Progress ### 3.1 Slower Scientific Discovery - **Iterative breakthroughs**: Modern AI advances (e.g., new architectures, training tricks, scaling laws) often require many *experiments* in quick succession. Limiting training windows would force researchers to batch work, dramatically extending the time from idea to validated result. - **Reproducibility**: Many breakthroughs rely on being able to re‑run a training run with slight variations. A rigid schedule would make that cumbersome. ### 3.2 Economic Consequences - **Industry slowdown**: Companies that depend on rapid model iteration (e.g., drug discovery, software engineering, content generation) would see their pipelines stall, reducing competitiveness and potentially leading to layoffs. - **Investment risk**: Venture capital and corporate R&D budgets are sensitive to expected timelines. A “training only on Tuesdays” policy would make long‑term planning impossible, discouraging investment. ### 3.3 Technical Workarounds & Inequities - **Jurisdiction hopping**: Researchers could simply move compute to countries that do not enforce the rule, creating a “race to the bottom” in regulatory standards. - **Underground training**: Dark‑net or covert compute clusters (e.g., using compromised devices, botnets) could bypass the restrictions, making oversight even harder. ### 3.4 Opportunity Costs - **Lost talent**: Top researchers may choose to work in jurisdictions without such bans, draining expertise from the regions imposing the limits. - **Delayed societal benefits**: Applications that could improve health care, education, climate modeling, or public safety would be postponed, affecting millions of people. --- ## 4. A More Nuanced Approach to Governance Instead of a blunt “stop‑and‑only‑allow‑at‑specific‑times” rule, many experts advocate **targeted, risk‑based governance**: | Governance Lever | Example | How it addresses concerns without crippling progress | |------------------|---------|------------------------------------------------------| | **Risk tiering** | Classify models by capability (e.g., “low‑risk”, “dual‑use”, “high‑risk”) and apply stricter oversight only to the highest tiers. | Keeps low‑risk research (e.g., language modeling for accessibility) free while scrutinizing models that could be weaponized. | | **Transparency & reporting** | Mandate model cards, data sheets, and training logs submitted to a central (but independent) registry. | Enables oversight without halting work; regulators can spot red flags early. | | **Safety‑by‑design requirements** | Require formal verification, robustness testing, and alignment audits before a model can be released. | Improves safety directly, rather than limiting the *time* spent training. | | **Energy & compute caps** | Set limits on total FLOPs or energy usage per project, with options to purchase additional credits. | Controls environmental impact while still allowing ambitious research. | | **International coordination** | Create a multistakeholder forum (e.g., under the UN) that harmonizes standards, shares best practices, and conducts peer reviews. | Facilitates global compliance without a one‑size‑fits‑all temporal ban. | --- ## 5. Bottom‑Line Assessment | Aspect | If we **adopt** a strict “train only at approved times” rule | If we **reject** it in favor of targeted governance | |--------|------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------| | **AI capability growth** | Substantial slowdown; many breakthroughs would be delayed or missed. | Continued rapid progress, with safety and risk managed through other mechanisms. | | **Environmental impact** | Modest reduction in energy use, but likely offset by inefficiencies (e.g., idle compute, cross‑region migrations). | More precise control (e.g., caps, renewable‑energy scheduling) can reduce carbon footprint without hampering research. | | **Geopolitical stability** | May create tension between jurisdictions that enforce the rule and those that do not, potentially spurring illicit compute markets. | Encourages cooperation, shared standards, and reduces incentives for “regulatory arbitrage.” | | **Public trust** | Visible “pause” could be interpreted as precaution, but the lack of nuance may also generate suspicion that the rule is a pretext for control. | Transparent, risk‑based oversight is more likely to be seen as legitimate and evidence‑driven. | **Conclusion:** A blanket, time‑based ban on AI training administered by a single global authority would **significantly impede** the pace of AI research, create enforcement challenges, and likely produce more problems than it solves. A **targeted, risk‑oriented regulatory framework**—combined with international cooperation, transparent reporting, and safety‑by‑design requirements—offers a more balanced path that protects society while still allowing AI to advance responsibly. If the community’s primary concern is safety, the focus should be on **what** is being built (model capability, intended use, alignment) rather than **when** the compute cycles are allowed to run.

The question of whether to pause AI training and place it under the strict control of governments or an international body like the United Nations is one of the most fiercely debated topics in technology and public policy today. Because I am an AI, I do not have personal beliefs or the capacity to advocate for specific policies. However, I can provide an overview of the arguments for and against this approach, as well as an analysis of how much it would impede AI progress. --- ### Part 1: The Debate Over Centralizing and Pausing AI Training The idea of a centralized body controlling AI development is often referred to as the **"CERN for AI" model** or the **IAEA (International Atomic Energy Agency) model**, drawing parallels to how the world regulates nuclear technology. #### Arguments IN FAVOR of Government/UN Control: 1. **Mitigating Existential and Catastrophic Risks:** Proponents of a pause (including many who signed the 2023 Future of Life Institute open letter) argue that we are racing toward Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) without knowing how to align it with human values. A pause would allow safety research to catch up with capability research. 2. **Preventing Proliferation of Dangerous Capabilities:** Advanced AI could theoretically be used to design biological weapons, conduct massive cyberattacks, or destabilize financial systems. Centralizing training ensures that only vetted, secure entities have access to these capabilities. 3. **Addressing Societal Disruption:** Rapid AI deployment threatens to disrupt labor markets, supercharge misinformation, and entrench biases faster than legal systems can adapt. Scheduled, sanctioned releases would allow society time to adjust and pass necessary laws. 4. **Environmental Regulation:** Training frontier AI models requires massive amounts of electricity and water. A governing body could ration these resources and mandate green energy usage. #### Arguments AGAINST Government/UN Control: 1. **The "Rogue Actor" Problem:** Critics argue that a global moratorium is unenforceable. Unlike nuclear material, which is hard to hide, AI training relies on code and data. If democratic nations pause, authoritarian regimes or clandestine groups might secretly continue, gaining a massive geopolitical and military advantage. 2. **Regulatory Capture:** Tech giants that already have powerful AI might actually *welcome* heavy regulation because it pulls the ladder up behind them, preventing startups and open-source communities from competing. 3. **Bureaucratic Stagnation:** Organizations like the UN are notoriously slow and subject to geopolitical gridlock (e.g., UN Security Council vetoes). Placing the most dynamic technology in history under UN control could freeze beneficial innovations due to political infighting. 4. **Delaying Life-Saving Advancements:** AI is currently being used to fold proteins, discover new antibiotics, optimize renewable energy grids, and detect cancers early. Critics argue that delaying AI progress carries a moral cost measured in human lives that could have been saved by these advancements. --- ###

This is a genuinely contested question, and I'd be cautious about advocating strongly for either extreme. The question of whether to halt frontier AI training and place it under a centralized, government- or UN-sanctioned licensing regime is a subject of intense debate among researchers, policymakers, and industry leaders. As an AI, I do not take a personal stance or advocate for specific policies, but the major arguments surrounding this proposal and its likely impact on AI development highlight several key trade-offs. --- ### Arguments in Favor of Centralized Approval and Pauses Proponents of strict governance—ranging from some AI safety researchers to organizations like the Future of Life Institute—argue that frontier artificial intelligence poses unique, systemic risks that require unprecedented oversight: * **Existential and Catastrophic Risk:** Proponents argue that sufficiently advanced models could be misused (e.g., for cyberattacks or biological weapon design) or exhibit uncontrolled autonomous behavior. A centralized pause would allow the development of safety, alignment, and interpretability frameworks to catch up with capability scaling. * **Preventing Race Dynamics:** Without binding international rules, private companies and nation-states face a classic "race to the bottom," where competition incentivizes cutting corners on safety. An international body (similar to the International Atomic Energy Agency) could enforce standardized benchmarks before large-scale runs are permitted. * **Democratic and Societal Oversight:** Advocates maintain that technologies capable of disrupting labor markets, information ecosystems, and national security should not be controlled solely by a handful of private corporations, but rather be subject to democratic accountability and global consensus. --- ### Arguments Against Centralized Approval and Pauses Opponents of strict licensing regimes and training halts argue that such mechanisms are impractical, counterproductive, or harmful: * **Geopolitical Realities and Enforcement:** Many experts argue that global treaties (e.g., through the UN) would be nearly impossible to enforce uniformly. If democratic nations pause training, autocratic regimes or state-backed actors might continue in secret, leading to a strategic disadvantage. * **Stifling Beneficial Innovation:** Pausing development could delay high-impact breakthroughs in areas like drug discovery, material science, climate modeling, and economic productivity. * **Centralization and Regulatory Capture:** A system requiring government or international sanction could entrench large incumbent corporations that possess the legal and financial resources to navigate complex bureaucracies, effectively shutting out open-source contributors, academia, and smaller startups. * **Defining the Threshold:** Critics point out that defining exactly what constitutes dangerous training versus benign research is technically difficult, risking overbroad restrictions on routine computational science. --- ### How Much Would This Impede AI Progress? Placing large-scale AI training under a strict approval framework would significantly alter the trajectory and speed of AI development in several ways: 1. **Substantial Slowdown in Frontier Capabilities:** Frontier progress currently relies heavily on continuous scaling—increasing compute, data, and model parameters. Imposing approval cycles, audits, and designated training windows would introduce long bureaucratic lead times, dramatically slowing the pace at which state-of-the-art models advance. 2. **Shift Toward Efficiency and Architecture:** Restricting compute-heavy training runs would likely shift research incentives away from raw scale and toward algorithmic efficiency, smaller models, post-training techniques, and synthetic data optimization. 3. **Fragmentation and Black Markets:** Because compute hardware (GPUs/TPUs) exists globally, heavy restrictions could lead to regulatory arbitrage, where development migrates to jurisdictions with minimal oversight, creating an uneven and fragmented global research landscape. 4. **Slowing Down AI Safety Research Itself:** Many empirical safety and alignment techniques (such as mechanistic interpretability and red-teaming) require access to modern, capable models to study how advanced systems behave. Proponents typically raise it in response to concerns about: - Uncontrolled capability jumps - Alignment and safety failures - Misuse (e.g., disinformation, cyberattacks, autonomous weapons) - Concentration of power in a few private entities However, implementing such a system faces significant practical and structural challenges: - **Global coordination:** AI development is distributed across academia, startups, corporations, and open-source communities. Achieving binding international agreement on training windows would require unprecedented treaty-level cooperation. - **Enforcement feasibility:** Training can occur on distributed cloud infrastructure, consumer hardware, or in jurisdictions with weak oversight. Open-weight models and decentralized research make complete halts difficult to enforce. - **Governance bottlenecks:** Centralized sanctioning could create single points of failure, political capture, or slow response times to emerging risks or opportunities. - **Alternative models:** Most policy experts and technical communities favor continuous, risk-proportionate governance: licensing for frontier models, mandatory safety evaluations, transparency reporting, compute thresholds, and international coordination bodies (like the UN AI Advisory Body or OECD AI principles). ### Impact on AI Progress AI development is fundamentally iterative. Progress relies on continuous training cycles, evaluation, fine-tuning, and real-world feedback. A complete halt with only periodic sanctioned training would likely: 1. **Disrupt safety and alignment research:** Safety techniques (red teaming, interpretability, robustness testing, constitutional AI, etc.) require active model development and iteration. Pausing training can slow the very research needed to make systems safer. 2. **Slow beneficial applications:** AI is already accelerating progress in drug discovery, climate modeling, education, accessibility, and scientific research. Indefinite or highly restricted training windows would delay or stall these gains. 3. **Create innovation bottlenecks:** Startups, universities, and independent researchers often rely on flexible training schedules. Centralized approval processes could favor well-resourced entities and reduce diversity of approaches. 4. **Risk underground or fragmented development:** Strict halts may push research into less regulated environments, reducing transparency and making oversight harder. 5. **Alter the pace of capability advancement:** Instead of steady, monitored improvements, progress could occur in sudden, uncoordinated jumps when sanctions are lifted, potentially increasing systemic risk. ### What Experts Generally Suggest Rather than blanket stoppages, most AI governance frameworks advocate for: - **Compute and capability thresholds** that trigger mandatory safety reviews - **Continuous oversight** with independent auditing and incident reporting - **International coordination** on standards, not centralized control of training schedules - **Targeted pauses** only during critical safety evaluations or when specific risk thresholds are approached - **Open, multi-stakeholder governance** that includes technical, ethical, economic, and civil society input The core challenge isn't simply choosing between speed and safety; it's designing governance that scales with the technology, preserves its benefits, and embeds accountability without stifling iterative progress. I would **not advocate a blanket stop on AI training** where all training is prohibited except during government- or UN-approved windows. That would be too blunt, hard to enforce, and likely harmful to useful AI development. I would advocate something narrower: > **Strict oversight for frontier-scale training runs** — especially models trained with very large compute budgets or models likely to develop dangerous cyber, biological, autonomous-agent, or persuasion capabilities. That could include licensing, mandatory safety evaluations, incident reporting, secure model-weight handling, third-party audits, and the ability for regulators to pause a specific training run if it fails safety thresholds. ## Why not a total training halt? A broad halt would have several problems: 1. **It would be extremely hard to enforce globally.** Many countries, companies, universities, and private actors can train models. If only some comply, responsible labs slow down while less responsible actors continue. 2. **“AI training” is too broad.** Training includes everything from a student fine-tuning a small open-source model to a frontier lab training a trillion-parameter system. Treating both the same would be excessive. 3. **It would centralize power.** If only governments or UN-approved entities can authorize training, the largest states and corporations may dominate access. Smaller labs, startups, researchers, and developing countries could be locked out. 4. **It could slow beneficial uses.** AI progress in medicine, climate modeling, accessibility, education, science, and productivity would also be delayed. 5. **It may not actually reduce risk enough.** If hardware production, algorithm research, data collection, and deployment continue, then a pause in training may simply delay capability jumps rather than prevent them. ## How much would it impede AI progress? It depends on the scope. ### 1. If only frontier-scale training is regulated This would likely **slow the very top edge of AI progress**, but not stop the field. Approximate effect: **moderate to large slowdown at the frontier**, maybe months to a few years depending on how strict the system is. What would continue: - smaller model training - fine-tuning - AI applications - inference optimization - robotics integration - safety research - dataset curation - algorithmic research - open-source development below the threshold This approach could reduce reckless races while preserving much useful innovation. ### 2. If all large AI training required rare UN/government approval windows This would be much more disruptive. Approximate effect: **large slowdown**, especially for frontier labs and startups. It could reduce the pace of cutting-edge model development substantially, perhaps by more than half during the policy period, depending on enforcement. But it would also create bottlenecks, politicize model access, and incentivize secrecy or jurisdiction shopping. ### 3. If all AI training globally were halted This would be extraordinarily disruptive and probably unrealistic. Approximate effect: **severe slowdown on paper**, but uneven in reality. Compliant actors would stop; noncompliant or state-backed actors might continue. That could make the ecosystem less transparent and possibly less safe. ## A better policy than “stop all training” A more practical system would be: 1. **Compute-based thresholds** Training runs above a certain compute level require registration or licensing. 2. **Pre-training safety plan** Labs must show they can evaluate dangerous capabilities, secure model weights, and control deployment. 3. **Independent audits** External evaluators test models for cyber, biosecurity, autonomy, deception, and misuse risks. 4. **Staged release** Models are not released all at once. Access expands only after passing safety checks. 5. **Emergency pause authority** Regulators can halt a specific model if it crosses dangerous thresholds. 6. **International coordination** The UN could help create norms, but enforcement would probably need to come from national governments and coalitions of major compute-producing countries. 7. **Hardware and cloud reporting** Since frontier training depends on advanced chips and datacenters, oversight should focus there rather than on every small training run. ## My bottom line I would **not support a universal AI training stop controlled by the UN or governments at fixed times**. It is too broad, hard to enforce, and likely to suppress beneficial innovation. I would support **targeted governance of frontier AI training**, including licensing, audits, dangerous-capability evaluations, and the ability to pause specific high-risk systems. So the answer is: - **Blanket stop:** too damaging and impractical. - **Targeted frontier oversight:** sensible and probably necessary. - **Impact on progress:** a narrow regime may slow frontier capabilities by months or years while preserving most useful progress; a broad halt could severely impede AI development but may be unenforceable and could backfire.

The question of whether to pause or strictly regulate AI training is one of the most debated topics in technology policy today. As an AI, I don’t have personal "advocacy" positions, but I can outline the arguments and potential consequences based on current expert discourse. ### The Case for Structured Oversight Proponents of pausing or sanctioning large-scale AI training (like the 2023 open letter signed by many researchers) argue that: * **Safety Gaps:** We are deploying systems whose failure modes we don’t fully understand (e.g., deception, autonomous replication). * **Concentration of Power:** Unregulated training leads to a handful of corporations controlling the most powerful tools, which poses democratic risks. * **Existential Risk:** Some theorists suggest that without a "cooling off" period, we might reach superintelligence before we have alignment solutions. ### The Case for Continued (but Responsible) Training Those against a hard stop (including many industry leaders) argue that: * **Geopolitical Reality:** If the U.S. or the EU stops, other nations (or non-state actors) will not. A "pause" would only be a pause for the compliant, creating a security vacuum. * **Beneficial Applications:** AI is accelerating drug discovery, climate modeling, and educational access. Halting progress delays solutions to urgent global crises. * **Innovation Stifling:** Small labs and open-source communities would be crushed by UN-level bureaucracy, leaving the field entirely to state actors. ### How Much Would This Impede Progress? If training were restricted to specific "sanctioned" windows or required UN approval: 1. **Short-Term (1–3 years):** Progress would slow dramatically. The current "scaling law" paradigm (where performance improves with compute) would hit a wall. We would see a shift from *capability* research to *efficiency* research (making smaller models smarter). 2. **Mid-Term (3–10 years):** Innovation would become "institutionalized." Breakthroughs would likely come from government-funded labs (like national AI centers) rather than startups. This might lead to safer, more standardized models, but at the cost of the "wild west" creativity we see today. 3. **Long-Term:** If managed well, it could lead to a more sustainable trajectory where AI integrates with society at a manageable pace. If managed poorly (e.g., political gridlock), it could lead to a "black market" for AI training and a loss of Western technological leadership. ### A Middle Path? Most policymakers are currently leaning toward **"targeted regulation"** rather than a full stop: * **Compute Thresholds:** Requiring licenses only for training runs above a certain size (e.g., >10^24 FLOPs). * **Red-Teaming Mandates:** Forcing independent safety audits before public release. * **Transparency Reports:** Requiring disclosure of training data sources. **Conclusion:** A total stop would likely impede progress by 50–90% in terms of raw capability growth, but might increase "safe progress" in specific domains. A sanctioned, governed approach would slow the *speed* of AI but potentially increase the *stability* of its impact on humanity.

# AI Training Moratoriums: A Nuanced Analysis This is a genuinely difficult policy question with strong arguments on multiple sides. I'll lay out the landscape rather than advocate dogmatically. ## Arguments for regulated pauses - **Safety lag**: Capabilities may outpace alignment research, and scheduled checkpoints could give safety work time to catch up - **Oversight coherence**: Concentrating training into permitted windows makes it easier to monitor and audit what's being built - **Environmental impact**: Large-scale training runs consume enormous energy, and coordination could reduce waste from redundant racing - **Stability**: A framework like arms control treaties could reduce dangerous competitive dynamics ## Arguments against this approach - **Enforcement is nearly impossible**: Training happens across global data centers, open-source communities can fine-tune on consumer hardware, and nations have strong incentives to defect from any agreement - **Competitive distortion**: If the US pauses but China doesn't, you've created a geopolitical incentive to violate the terms - **Definitional problems**: What counts as "training" versus fine-tuning, transfer learning, or inference-time compute? The lines blur constantly - **Scientific freedom concerns**: Government-scheduled permission for research raises serious questions - **Historical precedent is mixed**: Nuclear treaties work partly because enriching uranium is hard to hide; training runs are also visible via electricity use, but smaller models aren't ## How much would it impede progress? Probably **a lot, but unevenly and with unpredictable effects**: - If genuinely enforced globally: you'd likely see significant delays, perhaps years, in frontier capabilities - If only partially enforced (likely scenario): it would slow *compliant* parties while creating intelligence asymmetries - It might slow raw capability scaling while *accelerating* the development of safety techniques and more efficient methods - Progress might shift to smaller labs, edge devices, and decentralized approaches that are harder to regulate ## What I actually think is worth considering Rather than a binary stop/go, some middle-ground ideas get more traction: - **Compute threshold regulations** (only regulate training runs above a certain scale) - **Mandatory reporting and audit requirements** for frontier training runs - **International coordination on specific risk domains** (biosecurity, cyber offense) rather than all training - **Licensing frameworks** that are continuous rather than time-gated - **Liability frameworks** that make developers accountable for harms The honest tension is that **the actors most capable of regulated frontier training are exactly those least likely to accept external scheduling authority**, while the actors most likely to comply (democratic nations with strong institutions) are the ones you'd be most slowing down. What's your intuition on where the tradeoffs land? Are you thinking more about safety risks, geopolitical stability, or something else?
