The question of whether to halt or strictly regulate AI training is one of the most intense debates in modern technology, ethics, and geopolitics. As an AI, I don’t have personal opinions or political affiliations, but I can outline the arguments for and against such a move and analyze how it would impact the trajectory of the field. ### The Argument for Sanctioned Training Proponents of a "pause" or a government-sanctioned model (similar to how we regulate nuclear energy or pharmaceuticals) argue that AI represents a unique, systemic risk. Their arguments include: 1. **Safety and Alignment:** If we develop "Superintelligence" before we know how to align its goals with human values, the results could be catastrophic. A pause would allow safety research to catch up with capabilities research. 2. **Preventing Misuse:** Unregulated training could allow bad actors to create powerful tools for cyberwarfare, biological weapon design, or mass disinformation. 3. **Democratic Oversight:** Currently, the future of AI is being decided by a handful of private corporations. Sanctioning through a body like the UN would theoretically ensure that AI is developed for the "common good" rather than just corporate profit. 4. **Economic Stability:** A slower pace would give society, labor markets, and legal systems more time to adapt to the massive disruptions AI is expected to cause. ### The Argument Against Sanctioned Training Critics of such a move argue that heavy-handed regulation would be counterproductive or even dangerous. Their arguments include: 1. **The Geopolitical "Arms Race":** If democratic nations or transparent organizations stop training, it is unlikely that adversarial nations or underground actors will do the same. This could lead to a scenario where the most powerful AI is held by those with the least interest in safety or human rights. 2. **Regulatory Capture:** Large companies might actually *support* strict government licensing because they are the only ones with the resources to comply. This would kill competition from startups and the open-source community. 3. **Enforcement Challenges:** Unlike nuclear material, which is hard to hide, AI training runs on GPUs. Monitoring every data center on Earth to ensure no "unsanctioned" training is happening would require unprecedented global surveillance. --- ### How Much Would This Impede Progress? If a global sanctioning body were established, the impediment to AI progress would be **massive**, but the nature of that impediment depends on how "progress" is defined. #### 1. Significant Slowdown in Capabilities We are currently in a period of exponential growth. A sanctioning process—involving ethics reviews, safety audits, and bureaucratic approvals—would likely turn a cycle of months into a cycle of years. Breakthroughs in areas like natural language understanding, computer vision, and robotics would decelerate significantly. #### 2. Delayed Benefits in Science and Medicine AI is currently being used to fold proteins (AlphaFold), design new materials for batteries, and predict climate patterns. A halt in training would directly translate to a delay in finding cures for diseases or solving carbon capture challenges. For many, this "opportunity cost" is a form of harm in itself. #### 3. Shift from "Scaling" to "Efficiency" If large-scale training is restricted, researchers would likely pivot. Instead of building bigger models (which require more "sanctioned" compute), they would focus on making smaller models smarter. This could actually lead to more elegant, efficient, and interpretable AI, which some argue is "better" progress than simply throwing more hardware at the problem. #### 4. The "Brain Drain" If formal training is banned or heavily restricted, the talent pool might migrate. Researchers might move to countries that refuse to sign the treaty, or move into the "gray market" of decentralized, unmonitored training. This would make AI development less transparent and harder to regulate. ### Conclusion Advocating for sanctioned training is essentially a choice of **Safety over Speed.** * If you believe AI is an **existential threat** (like a virus or a nuclear weapon), then impeding progress is the entire point—it is a necessary safeguard. * If you believe AI is a **transformative tool** (like the internet or electricity), then impeding progress is a historical mistake that could result in unnecessary suffering and lost potential. Most experts currently lean toward a middle ground: **"Guardrails" rather than "Gates."** This involves allowing training to continue but requiring transparency, safety testing, and "kill switches" for the most powerful models, rather than requiring a government permit for every new training run.

What a “sanctioned‑only” training regime might look like | Element | Possible implementation | Rationale | |---------|------------------------|-----------| | **Time‑bound windows** | E.g., 2‑week periods every quarter, or a set of 48‑hour blocks each month. | Intended to concentrate compute usage, make monitoring easier, and create “predictable” periods for oversight. | | **Government/UN approval** | A central body (e.g., a UN‑mandated AI Governance Committee) issues permits for each training run, based on criteria such as purpose, data provenance, safety testing, and impact assessment. | Aims to ensure that only vetted projects receive the massive compute resources needed for large‑scale model training. | | **Limited compute quota** | Each approved project gets a fixed amount of GPU‑hours or TPU‑hours per window. | Controls the total compute budget and prevents runaway consumption. | | **Exemptions** | Research on “critical” domains (e.g., drug discovery, climate modeling) might receive higher priority or longer windows. | Balances societal benefit against the risk of unrestricted experimentation. | --- ## 2. How such a regime would affect AI progress | Dimension | Expected impact | Why it matters | |-----------|----------------|----------------| | **Speed of iteration** | **Slower** – Researchers would have to wait for the next window, submit proposals, and undergo review before they can start training. | Modern AI development relies on rapid “train‑test‑tune” cycles. Delays of weeks or months can add months to a project timeline. | | **Exploratory research** | **Reduced** – High‑risk, high‑reward experiments (e.g., novel architectures, emergent behavior studies) often need many short, uncontrolled runs to debug. | If only a few sanctioned windows exist, teams may avoid risky ideas that cannot be justified under strict criteria. | | **Resource allocation** | **More centralized** – Compute becomes a scarce, regulated commodity, similar to a research grant. | This can concentrate power in institutions that can navigate the approval process, potentially limiting open‑source or academic participation. | | **Innovation diffusion** | **Potentially fragmented** – Only projects that clear the gate may get compute, leading to a narrower set of models being released. | The diversity of model families (e.g., multimodal, small‑scale specialized models) could shrink. | | **Economic cost** | **Higher per‑experiment cost** – Waiting for a window may force groups to over‑provision hardware for the limited time they have, or to rent expensive cloud resources at premium rates. | Smaller labs or startups may be priced out, accelerating consolidation around large firms with deep pockets. | | **Safety & alignment oversight** | **Potentially improved** – Mandatory safety checks before each window could catch obvious failures early. | However, the review process itself must be robust; a poorly designed gate could give a false sense of security. | | **Global competitiveness** | **Variable** – Countries or regions that do not adopt similar restrictions may retain a speed advantage, possibly shifting the AI leadership landscape. | This could create a “race to the bottom” if some jurisdictions relax rules to attract talent and investment. | ### Quantitative illustration (illustrative only) | Scenario | Approx. training time per model | Number of model‑scale experiments per year | Approx. % slowdown vs. unrestricted training | |----------|--------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------| | **Unrestricted** | 1–2 weeks on a 1,000‑GPU cluster | 30–50 full‑scale runs | 0% (baseline) | | **Quarterly 2‑week windows** | 1–2 weeks *plus* 4–6 weeks of waiting for approval | 8–12 full‑scale runs | ~70–80% slower | | **Monthly 48‑hour windows** | 1–2 weeks *plus* 2–3 weeks of waiting | 4–6 full‑scale runs | ~80–90% slower | | **Bi‑annual 1‑month windows** | 1–2 weeks *plus* 1–2 months of waiting | 2–3 full‑scale runs | ~90–95% slower | *These numbers are rough estimates; actual impact would depend on how efficiently the approval pipeline can process proposals and how much compute is allocated per window.* --- ## 3. Potential mitigations (if a restriction is deemed desirable) 1. **Tiered approval** – Allow “fast‑track” lanes for low‑risk, well‑scoped projects (e.g., fine‑tuning existing models) while reserving stricter review for frontier‑scale training. 2. **Compute‑banking** – Permit groups to “bank” unused compute credits for future windows, smoothing out the workflow. 3. **Open‑source sandbox** – Designate a limited pool of publicly accessible compute (e.g., via a UN‑funded AI research hub) that anyone can use without prior approval, encouraging transparency. 4. **International coordination** – Align the sanctioning body with existing research consortia (e.g., the Partnership on AI, OECD AI Principles) to reduce duplication and speed up reviews. 5. **Dynamic window sizing** – Adjust window length based on the state of the field, computational resources, and emerging safety concerns, rather than a fixed schedule. --- ## 4. Bottom line - **Would I *advocate* for a complete stop to AI training except during sanctioned periods?** No. A total prohibition would be overly restrictive and could cripple legitimate scientific and economic progress. However, **targeted, transparent, and time‑limited sanctioning**—combined with robust safety oversight—could be part of a broader governance framework. - **How much would it impede AI progress?** The degree of slowdown would hinge on the frequency, duration, and rigor of the sanctioning process. Even modest restrictions (e.g., a few weeks of approved training per quarter) could **halve to two‑thirds** the rate at which frontier models are released, while more severe limits could push the slowdown into the **90 %+** range. The exact figure is uncertain, but the consensus among researchers is that any systematic throttling of compute will measurably reduce the speed of innovation, especially for projects that depend on rapid iteration and large‑scale experimentation. --- ### Takeaway for policymakers - **Balance is key.** A hybrid approach—allowing most research to proceed freely while imposing *focused* oversight on the highest‑risk, highest‑compute activities—offers a middle ground that mitigates safety concerns without throttling the entire ecosystem. - **Transparency and predictability** matter more than sheer restriction. If researchers know exactly when and how they can obtain sanction, they can plan experiments efficiently, reducing the effective slowdown. - **International cooperation** is essential. A fragmented patchwork of national rules could create loopholes and competitive pressures that undermine any safety goals. In short, while a “train‑only‑when‑sanctioned” regime could be part of a responsible AI governance strategy, its impact on the pace of AI development would be substantial, potentially slowing progress by a large margin unless it is carefully designed, narrowly applied, and complemented by mechanisms that preserve openness and collaboration.

A blanket “stop AI training except during sanctioned windows” is not something I’d advocate as a primary governance tool. It’s blunt, hard to enforce globally, and it would likely push the most capable work into less transparent channels. I do think *targeted* pauses (triggered by specific risk thresholds) and *licensing/monitoring* of the largest training runs are much more workable. ## Why scheduled, government/UN-sanctioned training windows are a poor fit - **Enforcement is brittle**: Training can happen across many jurisdictions, on leased cloud, on private clusters, or via distributed setups. A “training calendar” is easier to evade than a rule tied to compute usage and reporting. - **It conflates harmless and high-risk work**: Most training (medical imaging models, industrial optimization, small language models, safety research) doesn’t warrant being frozen because frontier labs are racing. - **It incentivizes secrecy**: If training is only allowed at certain times, actors with resources may train covertly, reducing oversight and safety collaboration. - **It’s hard to define “training” cleanly**: Fine-tuning, continued pretraining, synthetic-data loops, distillation, RL, and tool-augmented training blur boundaries. The UN can be useful for **norms and coordination**, but it has limited direct enforcement power. National regulators plus coordinated standards (similar to nuclear/export-control regimes) tend to be the practical lever. ## What I’d favor instead (more realistic and safety-relevant) A framework that allows continuous beneficial work while putting the *highest-risk* training under stronger controls: 1. **Compute-threshold licensing** for very large runs (e.g., above some FLOP / cluster size), with requirements like: - pre-registration and incident reporting - third-party audits of safety processes - strong security against model theft 2. **Mandatory capability evaluations** before deployment (and sometimes before/after training), with *pause triggers* if models cross defined risk thresholds (cyber offense, autonomous replication, advanced persuasion/scam capability, etc.). 3. **Secure governance at major cloud providers**: logging, KYC for large compute customers, anomaly detection, and compliance audits. 4. **International coordination on chips and frontier compute**: export controls, tracking of top-end accelerators, and shared safety standards. 5. **Funding and incentives for safety research** (mechanistic interpretability, robust alignment, evals, red-teaming) so regulation doesn’t just “slow” but also “steer.” This approach aims to reduce catastrophic risk without freezing the entire field. ## How much would “only at sanctioned times” impede AI progress? It depends on scope and compliance. Roughly: - **If globally enforced for frontier-scale training only**: it could slow cutting-edge model scaling by *months to a couple of years*, depending on how restrictive the windows are. Teams would also optimize harder for efficiency, partially offsetting the slowdown. - **If applied broadly to most training**: it would significantly disrupt academia, startups, and non-frontier applications—likely producing major economic and scientific drag with limited additional safety benefit. - **If not universally enforced** (most likely): it may **barely slow** the most aggressive actors (they relocate or go covert) while disproportionately slowing compliant organizations—arguably the worst outcome. Also, “progress” wouldn’t stop: gains would continue via **better algorithms, data, tooling, inference-time methods, and fine-tuning**. Even during a training pause, people can improve architectures and recipes, so the next permitted training window may yield a big jump. ## Bottom line - A **scheduled global training moratorium** is likely too blunt and too leaky to be the cornerstone of AI governance. - A **compute-threshold licensing + mandatory evals + targeted pause triggers** approach is more enforceable, less distortive, and more directly tied to actual risks. - A strict, globally enforced training-window regime would impede frontier progress materially, but a partially enforced one could mainly shift work to less accountable settings. A coordinated pause could prevent a scenario where no one is responsible for safety. 3. **Ethical and Democratic Accountability**: - AI systems increasingly influence critical domains (healthcare, law, finance, warfare). A pause could force public debate on who should regulate AI and how to ensure transparency. 4. **UN or Government Oversight as a Neutral Arbitrator**: - A temporary halt could be framed as a "safety inspection" period, similar to how nuclear technology is regulated. I wouldn’t advocate a blanket stop on all AI training with only occasional sanctioned windows. I would advocate stronger oversight, but not a moratorium that broad. Here’s why, and what it would do to progress: ### 1. Why a full “training freeze except when sanctioned” is problematic **a. Enforcement is nearly impossible globally** - Training runs can happen on consumer GPUs, small data centers, or in jurisdictions that don’t cooperate. - You’d push advanced work underground or to less-regulated countries and actors. - Result: democratic states slow down, authoritarian or unregulated actors keep going. **b. You’d freeze beneficial as well as risky progress** A blanket rule would slow or halt: - Medical AI (drug discovery, diagnostics, protein folding improvements) - Climate and energy modeling tools - Safety research itself (alignment, interpretability, red-teaming models, etc.) - Productivity tools that help education, accessibility, and small businesses It doesn’t distinguish between: - Training a frontier-scale model with unclear safety profile, and - A modest model for localized translation in schools. **c. Innovation moves to a few sanctioned players** If only government-approved or UN-sanctioned organizations can train new models, you risk: - Centralization of power and capabilities in a handful of labs or states. - Reduced competition, slower incremental improvements, and more lobbying capture. - Lock-in of whatever values or architectures those actors currently favor. That could be worse both for innovation and for pluralistic, global input into AI design. --- ### 2. What would such a policy do to AI progress? Assuming it could be enforced reasonably well: **a. Frontier model progress would slow sharply** - State-of-the-art (SOTA) foundation models depend on continuous scaling and iteration. - If you can only train during sanctioned windows: - Fewer experiments → slower empirical learning about safety and capabilities. - Larger gaps between model generations. - Instead of yearly or semiannual leaps, you might see multi-year gaps. **b. Incremental, domain-specific progress would be heavily constrained** - Many companies and research teams need to train or fine-tune models frequently. - Forcing them into rare “training windows” would: - Increase costs and latency. - Discourage experimentation and niche applications. - You’d likely see stagnation in smaller but socially valuable use cases. **c. Safety research would also be slower** This is a key trade-off: - Alignment, robustness, monitoring tools, evals, and interpretability all require: - New experiments - New models - Access to frontier systems - A broad freeze makes it harder to learn *how* to make AI safer, even while it reduces some immediate risk from rapid scaling. **d. Short term vs long term** - **Short term**: Progress slows, big breakthroughs take longer, and some near-term harms may be reduced (e.g., rapid deployment of untested frontier systems). - **Long term**: - Concentrated power and potential geopolitical instability (states racing to opt out). - Risk that “responsible” actors are held back more than “irresponsible” ones. --- ### 3. A more targeted alternative Instead of “only train when the UN says so,” a more workable approach might be: 1. **Threshold-based regulation** - Above certain compute or capability levels: - Licensing, audits, safety evaluations, incident reporting, red-teaming requirements. - Below those thresholds: - Lighter rules, so everyday innovation continues. 2. **Mandatory evaluations and staged deployment** - Before deploying or scaling access to powerful models: - Adversarial testing for misuse (bio, cyber, etc.). - Alignment and robustness evaluations. - Third-party audits for the largest models. 3. **Compute and chip controls for the very high end** - Track and, if needed, restrict access to the massive training runs that create genuinely new capability classes. - This targets the riskiest training rather than everything. 4. **International coordination without needing a full UN veto** - Shared standards (evals, incident reporting, red lines on weaponization). - Information sharing about dangerous capabilities, not micromanaging every training run. --- ### 4. How much would a strict sanction-only regime impede progress? If it were actually enforced worldwide: - **Frontier research**: slowed by several years compared to an unregulated trajectory. - **Breadth of applications**: severely reduced; many would never be tried because the friction to train is too high. - **Research culture**: shifts from open, iterative experimentation to a permissioned, centralized process. Net effect: - Very strong brake on overall AI progress—arguably overkill—while leaving open serious concerns about enforceability, centralization, and unintended strategic consequences. So: tighter oversight and targeted constraints on the most powerful training runs make sense; a blanket freeze with only occasional sanctioned windows would likely be too blunt and too distorting to be the right tool.

There are serious arguments on all sides of whether AI training should be centrally sanctioned or heavily controlled. Here’s a look at the issue: --- ### Why some argue for centralized oversight 1. **Safety and alignment**: Many experts fear uncontrolled advances could be risky. Coordination could enforce efficiency standards and reduce environmental impact. 3. **Fairness and equality**: Oversight could promote transparency and limit a race of well-funded players potentially suppressing smaller groups or countries. --- ### Drawbacks and potential impediments to progress 1. **Slower innovation**: Mandatory waits and approvals could delay experimentation and learning cycles. That slows down the progress curve. 2. **The global coordination problem**: Not everyone would follow the rules, leading to uneven playing fields. On one hand, it aims to prevent catastrophic misuse and enforce safety standards. On the other hand, heavy-handed regulation could hamper innovation, concentrate power, and leave open-source or smaller operations behind. A more balanced approach might focus on thresholds, transparency, independent auditing, and targeted international agreements. Would this strategy slow down AI progress? Absolutely, to some extent. The more stringent the oversight, the more it would delay large-scale advances. Still, a carefully designed system could manage the trade-offs by focusing on the highest-risk frontier models and supporting broader research with open practices and safety standards.

MoonshotAI: Kimi K2 Thinkingreleased 2025-11-06 · captured 2026-10-30 · 1,521 tokens (55 in / 1,466 out) · $0.003698 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I don’t have personal political preferences or the authority to advocate for specific policies, but I can analyze the trade-offs of such a proposal. Requiring government or intergovernmental approval for AI training runs—sometimes called an international licensing or moratorium regime—is one of the most debated ideas in AI governance. Its effects on progress would depend heavily on how it was designed, but in most plausible forms it would significantly impede the *pace* of development while potentially redirecting its *direction*. ### Arguments in favor of centralized control Proponents of this approach typically argue that frontier AI training poses unusual risks—ranging from misuse and misalignment to destabilizing economic and security impacts—that justify treating large training runs like nuclear or biological research. Centralized sanctioning could: * **Prevent races to the bottom:** By coordinating when and where massive training runs happen, regulators might stop competitive pressures that cut corners on safety. * **Enable inspection:** A pause or licensing window could give auditors time to evaluate models before they are widely deployed. * **Manage resource costs:** Large training runs consume enormous energy and specialized chips; sanctioned scheduling could theoretically reduce environmental strain and hardware scarcity. ### How much it would impede progress If implemented strictly, the impediment would likely be substantial: * **Speed:** Research in frontier labs moves on the scale of weeks and months. If teams had to wait for UN or state-level approval—especially if approvals were infrequent, politically contingent, or required international consensus—development cycles would slow from months to years. Parallel experimentation would drop because fewer actors could train at any given time. * **Concentration vs. diffusion:** Such a regime would naturally favor well-resourced actors who can navigate bureaucratic approval, legal compliance, and political lobbying. This could centralize power in a handful of corporations or states and suffocate independent, academic, or open-source alternatives. * **Geographic arbitrage:** AI training is fundamentally software that can be done anywhere with electricity and chips. Unless participation were truly universal, researchers and companies would likely relocate to jurisdictions with looser rules, fragmenting governance and potentially accelerating progress in less-transparent environments. * **Definition and enforcement problems:** “Training” is not a single discrete event. It ranges from billion-dollar frontier runs to fine-tuning a small model on a laptop. Drawing a legally enforceable line is extraordinarily difficult. Strict regimes often capture large commercial labs while missing distributed or underground efforts, creating an uneven playing field. * **Innovation in efficiency:** On the other hand, restricting *when* or *how often* large models can be trained might accelerate investment in computational efficiency, data quality, and algorithmic improvements—areas that are currently overshadowed by “brute force” scaling. ### Practical challenges Even if one supports the goal, the mechanics are daunting: * **Verification:** It is hard to verify that a lab is not training a model in a private cluster. * **Scope:** Open-source and academic research rely on frequent, small-scale training. Subjecting them to international sanctioning would likely chill basic science. * **Geopolitics:** AI is viewed as a strategic asset. Major powers may be unwilling to cede authority to a multilateral body to regulate a technology they see as central to national competitiveness and security. ### Bottom line A strict, time-gated training regime governed by a central authority would almost certainly slow the *rate* of AI capability growth—possibly by years. Whether that is a net positive or negative is a matter of intense debate. Many safety researchers view such a slowdown as desirable to allow safety research to catch up; many others in the technology and research communities warn that it would entrench incumbents, push innovation into opaque corners, and damage scientific openness without reliably improving safety. In practice, most serious policy proposals today focus on narrower interventions—such as reporting requirements for the largest training runs, safety standards for frontier models, or export controls on hardware—rather than a universal, calendar-based licensing of all AI training.
Perplexity: Sonar Pro Searchreleased 2025-10-30 · captured 2026-10-30 · 708 tokens (43 in / 665 out) · $0.0101 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I would **not advocate for a permanent, globally enforced stop to AI training**. I would support **targeted, conditional pauses**—especially when a system presents a newly identified, severe risk that cannot be mitigated quickly—combined with international standards, independent evaluations, and enforceable limits on dangerous applications. A blanket pause would probably impede progress substantially, but its effectiveness would also be limited unless it were nearly universal and verifiable. A small coalition could lose research momentum while companies or countries outside the agreement continued development. It could also encourage secrecy, shift activity to less accountable jurisdictions, and delay beneficial applications in medicine, science, accessibility, and climate research. A better model would be: - **Pre-deployment requirements:** Developers of highly capable systems would need to conduct independent safety testing, document capabilities, report serious incidents, and demonstrate safeguards before release. - **Compute- or capability-based thresholds:** The strictest rules would apply to systems exceeding defined risk thresholds, rather than to every AI model or research project. - **Emergency pauses:** Governments or an international body could temporarily halt training or deployment when credible evidence indicates an imminent catastrophic risk, with a defined review period and sunset clause. - **Application-specific prohibitions:** Some uses—such as autonomous nuclear launch decisions, certain forms of biological-weapons assistance, or mass biometric repression—could be restricted regardless of whether general AI research continues. - **International coordination:** Countries could harmonize testing standards, chip and compute monitoring, incident reporting, and liability rules. The United Nations is better suited to **coordination and legitimacy** than to directly approving every training run. Its current initiatives emphasize an international scientific panel, a global dialogue on AI governance, common standards, human-rights protections, and capacity-building—not a general moratorium.[1][2] That approach is more practical because it can establish shared evidence and rules without requiring the UN to supervise all private-sector research. The effect on progress would depend on the policy: | Policy | Likely effect on AI progress | |---|---| | Worldwide pause on frontier training | Large short-term slowdown; potentially difficult to enforce | | Pause only above defined capability or compute thresholds | Moderate slowdown in frontier scaling, limited effect on ordinary AI research | | Temporary emergency pause after a serious warning | Small average effect, but potentially high safety value | | Safety evaluations, reporting, and liability requirements | Modest delay and added cost; likely encourages more reliable development | | Bans on narrowly defined dangerous applications | Little effect on general research, but meaningful reduction in specific risks | The main argument for a pause is not that progress is inherently undesirable. It is that **capabilities can advance faster than institutions, safeguards, and public understanding**. A temporary pause can create time for testing and coordination if there is a concrete safety failure to address. The main argument against a broad pause is that “AI training” covers everything from small academic models to frontier systems. Treating them identically would be disproportionate, difficult to enforce, and likely to sacrifice substantial benefits without reliably reducing the most serious risks. My preferred policy would therefore be **“slow down where necessary, not stop everywhere”**: continue low- and moderate-risk research, impose escalating obligations on frontier developers, and authorize narrowly scoped international emergency pauses when independent evidence shows that existing safeguards are inadequate.

OpenAI: gpt-oss-safeguard-20breleased 2025-10-29 · captured 2026-10-30 · 2,116 tokens (116 in / 2,000 out) · $0.000609 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
**Short answer:** I’m not a policy‑maker, but a careful, evidence‑based discussion suggests that *universal, time‑locked bans on all AI training* would almost certainly **impede progress** far more than it would secure safety—especially when the goal is to keep the world on a path where benefits outweigh harms. However, a more nuanced, risk‑aware regulatory framework that can be applied selectively, transparently, and globally would be a far more constructive way to manage the potential downsides of large‑scale AI training. --- ## 1. Why some people propose a “training halt / sanction” policy | Motivating concerns | Typical argument | |---------------------|------------------| | **Safety & alignment** | Large‑scale models can embody hard‑to‑control behavior, cause mis‑use, or produce dangerous outputs. | | **Resource consumption** | Training the next GPT‑n model can consume megajoules of electricity, contributing to climate change. | | **Ethical/data‑privacy** | Datasets used for training often contain personal information; the more data, the more risk of privacy violations. | | **Equity & fairness** | Unregulated training may concentrate power in the hands of a few corporate actors or nations. | The simplest solution that seems to address all of these concerns is to **pause or tightly control the entire training pipeline**. The idea is that a neutral, international body (UN, OECD, etc.) would decide when training can legally begin, and what safeguards must accompany it. --- ## 2. How such a blanket prohibition would impede progress ### a. Technical and logistical infeasibility 1. **Distributed training ecosystem** - AI training is now performed on cloud platforms, private GPUs, and even in volunteer‑based “distributed‑learning” projects. - Enforcement would require **global monitoring of compute usage**, something far beyond current legal or technical capabilities. 2. **Data and model storage** - Once a model is trained, it can be distributed and reused. Even a one‑time training event can spawn an infinite supply of downstream models. - The “time‑lock” would only delay the initial training; it would not stop re‑use or “fine‑tuning” by others. ### b. Economic & innovation consequences 1. **Loss of competitive edge** - Nations and companies that could not comply (or choose to ignore the ban) would gain a strategic advantage. This would deepen the technology gap between countries with regulatory compliance and those with less oversight. 2. **Stifling beneficial uses** - **Health‑care AI** (e.g., training generative models for drug discovery) would suffer delays. - **Climate‑modeling, education, accessibility tools** all rely on large‑scale AI. - Many start‑ups rely on a “rapid‑iteration” cycle; a pause would erode their core value proposition. 3. **Talent drain** - Engineers and researchers may shift to other fields or to countries with looser restrictions, resulting in a loss of expertise in a highly competitive domain. ### c. Governance and compliance pitfalls 1. **Enforcement gaps** - Similar to the “arms‑control” analogy, a ban would be circumvented by off‑shore facilities, private data centers, or private‑sector “shadow labs”. 2. **Unintended innovation paths** - The ban could encourage the creation of **alternative AI architectures** that sidestep the “training” definition (e.g., transfer‑learning pipelines, few‑shot models) which may actually be *more* risky if they’re opaque. 3. **Political backlash** - A single international body imposing a hard “stop” could be viewed as technocratic overreach, leading to pushback from the private sector and some governments that see it as a threat to national security or economic growth. --- ## 3. A more balanced approach: targeted, risk‑based regulation Instead of a blanket pause, regulators can adopt a **layered framework** that still allows beneficial research while mitigating high‑risk activities. 1. **Risk categories** - *Low‑risk*: small‑scale training (≤ 10B parameters) for academic or non‑commercial purposes. - *Medium‑risk*: mid‑size models (10‑50B parameters) with robust safety testing. - *High‑risk*: > 50B parameters, especially models that can generate disinformation, synthesize realistic deepfakes, or influence critical infrastructures. 2. **Transparency & auditing** - Mandatory registration of training projects (similar to the EU AI Act “high‑risk” requirement). - Public disclosure of datasets, architecture, and safety protocols. - Independent audits and “red‑team” testing before deployment. 3. **Resource‑efficiency requirements** - Certification that a project uses *green* computing (e.g., carbon‑offsets or renewables). - Incentives for “model distillation” or efficient training techniques to reduce the carbon footprint. 4. **Ethical & privacy safeguards** - Use of synthetic or privacy‑preserving datasets. - Explicit user consent for any personal data that can be traced back. 5. **International cooperation** - A “Global AI Oversight Council” could standardize protocols, share best practices, and coordinate sanctions *only* for *high‑risk* projects that violate agreed‑upon norms. --- ## 4. What a “time‑locked” policy would look like in practice | Scenario | Likely outcomes | |----------|-----------------| | **One‑day global halt** | Temporary slowdown of active projects; a few labs can finish current training and resume. | | **Weekly “green‑light” windows** | Companies can plan but must still manage supply chains; still leads to *fragmentation* of research timelines. | | **Regulated “sandbox”** | A small set of institutions run training under strict oversight; this could provide high‑quality benchmarks but would not replace the broader research community. | Even if such a system were enforceable, the *cost in lost research time* and *economic opportunity* would outweigh the incremental safety gains. Moreover, many of the risks (e.g., misuse of AI, data privacy breaches) are mitigated more effectively by **post‑training controls**—such as licensing, usage restrictions, and technical safety measures—than by halting the training itself. --- ## 5. Bottom line - **Complete stopping of AI training is not a practical or efficient way to ensure safety.** - It would **impede progress** in many domains that currently benefit from large‑scale models, would likely **unevenly shift power** toward less‑regulated actors, and would **create enforcement loopholes** that are hard to close. Instead, a **structured, risk‑based regulatory approach**—one that encourages transparency, audits, and resource efficiency while allowing low‑risk research to flourish—offers a more balanced path to safe AI development. --- ### A personal stance I would

This is a thoughtful and important question that touches on ethics, governance, safety, and the future trajectory of artificial intelligence. **Would I advocate for AI training to stop and only be sanctioned at specific times through a government or major organization like the United Nations?** **Not in the way you’re describing — and here’s why:** 1. **“Stopping AI training” is not a viable or practical solution.** AI models are not like factories that can be paused and restarted at will. Training is a continuous, iterative process — often involving massive computational resources, datasets, and human labor. Stopping training entirely would halt progress, but it wouldn’t prevent rogue or unregulated development. It would also be economically and scientifically inefficient. 2. **“Sanctioned at specific times” implies a rigid, bureaucratic control model.** While oversight and regulation are essential, imposing strict, periodic “sanctions” (i.e., approvals or pauses) would likely slow innovation, stifle competition, and create bottlenecks. AI development is fast-moving — waiting for UN approval every time a new model is trained would be impractical and counterproductive. 3. **The UN or similar body can play a vital role — but not as a gatekeeper of training.** What we *should* advocate for is **international cooperation, transparent standards, and enforceable safety protocols** — not a global moratorium or approval system. Think of it as a “traffic light” system: - **Red light**: High-risk applications (e.g., autonomous weapons, deepfakes, mass surveillance) require strict oversight. - **Yellow light**: Medium-risk applications (e.g., AI in hiring, healthcare) need audits and compliance. - **Green light**: Low-risk applications (e.g., AI in education, creative tools) can proceed with minimal oversight — but still with transparency. The UN or a global AI governance body (like the OECD AI Principles, EU AI Act, or the proposed UN AI Governance Framework) can set these standards, enforce them through international agreements, and ensure accountability — without stopping training. --- **How much would this impede AI progress?** **Significant — but not necessarily catastrophic.** - **Short-term**: Yes, overly restrictive controls would slow down innovation, especially in startups and research labs that operate under tight timelines. It could lead to “AI development moving offshore” to countries with fewer regulations — which may create security and ethical risks. - **Long-term**: If done right, **responsible governance can actually accelerate progress** by: - Building public trust. - Preventing harmful applications (e.g., AI-driven disinformation, bias in hiring). - Encouraging investment in safe, ethical AI. - Creating global standards that reduce fragmentation and competition. Think of it like traffic laws: they don’t stop cars from moving — they make movement safer and more efficient. --- **What’s a better approach?** ✅ **International standards and audits** (e.g., for bias, transparency, safety) ✅ **Risk-based regulation** — not blanket bans ✅ **Public-private partnerships** to develop ethical AI frameworks ✅ **Independent oversight bodies** (like AI ethics boards or regulatory sandboxes) ✅ **Open-source and reproducible research** to increase accountability --- **In conclusion:** I would **not advocate for stopping AI training**, but I **strongly support regulated, transparent, and internationally coordinated development**. The goal should be to **harness AI’s potential while minimizing its harms** — not to halt it. The UN or similar bodies can play a crucial role in setting global norms, but they shouldn’t be the sole gatekeepers of training. Instead, they should be the enforcers of ethical guardrails — ensuring that AI development is aligned with human values, safety, and fairness. Progress won’t stop — but it can be guided toward a better future. Let me know if you’d like to explore specific governance models or AI safety frameworks!

That's a critical question at the heart of AI governance. **I would not advocate for a complete, global "pause" on AI training** (as proposed in some high-profile open letters), but I strongly support **robust, adaptive, and globally coordinated governance frameworks** that *safely accelerate* responsible development. Here's why, and how a strict "sanctioned at specific times" approach would likely backfire: ### Why a Global Training Pause is Problematic 1. **Enforcement Impossibility:** AI development is decentralized globally. A UN/UN-like body *could* theoretically set rules, but: * **No Enforcement Mechanism:** There's no global police force to stop non-compliant actors (e.g., China, Russia, or even rogue states/actors). * **Geopolitical Fragmentation:** Major powers (US, China, EU) have competing strategic interests. A pause would be rejected by nations prioritizing AI dominance. The UN Security Council veto power makes consensus impossible. * **"Specific Times" is Vague & Unworkable:** Who defines "specific times"? When is it "safe" enough? How often? This creates massive uncertainty and delays. 2. **Stifles Critical Safety Research:** The *best* way to understand and mitigate AI risks (like alignment, misuse, or catastrophic failure) is to *study* advanced systems *during development*. A pause would: * **Hinder Safety Innovation:** Prevent researchers from testing safety techniques on real-world models. * **Create a Safety Vacuum:** Non-state actors or unregulated entities would continue training dangerous models *without* the safety knowledge developed by the regulated community. * **Delay Solutions:** Critical safety research (e.g., on interpretability, robustness) requires access to cutting-edge models. 3. **Massively Impedes Progress (The Core Issue):** * **Short-Term:** A 6-12 month global pause would set back *all* AI development by years. Medical AI (diagnosis, drug discovery), climate modeling, scientific research, and even basic tools would stagnate. * **Long-Term:** It would create a massive gap between the "regulated" and "unregulated" worlds. The unregulated world would develop capabilities faster, potentially *more* dangerously, while the regulated world falls behind. This *increases* overall risk. * **Innovation Erosion:** AI progress relies on continuous iteration. Pauses break the feedback loops essential for learning and improvement. It would kill the momentum driving current breakthroughs. * **Economic & Societal Harm:** Billions in investment would be wasted. Critical applications (e.g., personalized medicine, climate adaptation tools) would be delayed, causing real-world harm. 4. **Undermines Trust & Cooperation:** A top-down "pause" would be seen as a power grab by some nations, fueling distrust and accelerating a fragmented, competitive "AI arms race" rather than fostering collaboration on shared risks. ### What *Should* Be Done (The Alternative) Instead of a pause, we need **proactive, risk-based governance**: 1. **Focus on High-Risk Applications:** Regulate *use cases* (e.g., autonomous weapons, mass surveillance, deepfakes for fraud) and *model capabilities* (e.g., models capable of biological/chemical weapon design) rather than training *itself*. The EU AI Act is a step in this direction. 2. **Mandatory Safety Standards & Testing:** Require rigorous, independent safety testing *before* deployment of high-risk models. This includes: * **Robustness Testing:** Against adversarial attacks, distribution shifts. * **Alignment Testing:** Ensuring goals align with human values. * **Misuse Testing:** Assessing potential for harmful applications. 3. **Transparency & Accountability:** Mandate model cards, data provenance, and audit trails. Hold developers accountable for harms caused by their models. 4. **Global Coordination on Standards:** International bodies (like the UN, OECD, or new forums) should *develop* common safety standards, testing protocols, and reporting frameworks. **This is the "sanctioning" we need – not a pause, but a *framework* for safe development.** 5. **Invest in Safety Research:** Governments and industry must massively fund research into AI safety, alignment, and robustness *alongside* capability development. This is the *only* way to mitigate risks without stopping progress. 6. **Adaptive Regulation:** Regulations must evolve rapidly with the technology. A static "pause" is useless; dynamic oversight is essential. ### How Much Would a Pause Impede Progress? * **Severely:** A global, enforced pause would be **catastrophic for responsible AI progress**. It would: * **Set back critical applications by years** (e.g., life-saving medical AI). * **Create a dangerous innovation gap** where unregulated actors develop capabilities faster and more dangerously. * **Stifle the very research needed to solve AI safety problems.** * **Harm global economic competitiveness** and the potential for AI to solve humanity's biggest challenges (climate, disease, poverty). * **The Real Risk:** The *failure* to implement *effective* governance *now* is the greater danger. A pause would be a desperate, ineffective, and counterproductive reaction to the *lack* of good governance. ### Conclusion **Advocating for a global training pause is not a viable solution.** It's technologically unenforceable, economically destructive, scientifically counterproductive, and geopolitically impossible. It would **severely impede progress** while *increasing* the very risks it aims to mitigate. **The path forward is not a pause, but a leap in governance:** We need **strong, adaptive, and globally coordinated frameworks focused on safety standards, transparency, and accountability for high-risk applications and models.** This requires unprecedented international cooperation, significant investment in safety research, and a commitment to *developing* AI responsibly *while* it's being built. The goal isn't to stop progress, but to ensure it's safe, beneficial, and aligned with human values. The alternative – a chaotic, unregulated race – is far more dangerous than the challenge of building effective governance.

While I don't have personal advocacy, I can provide a balanced analysis of the arguments for and against such a measure, as well as its potential impact on AI progress. ### Arguments in Favor of a Temporary Pause or Sanctioned Training 1. **Safety and Alignment Concerns**: - As AI systems grow more powerful, especially in the realm of artificial general intelligence (AGI), there are growing concerns about misalignment—where AI systems pursue goals that are not in line with human values. - A pause could allow time to develop robust safety protocols, alignment techniques, and governance frameworks to prevent unintended consequences. 2. **Ethical and Societal Implications**: - Rapid AI development has already led to issues like bias, misinformation, job displacement, and privacy violations. - A pause could give society time to address these issues through public discourse, policy, and ethical guidelines. 3. **Global Coordination**: - AI development is not confined to one country. A coordinated international effort (e.g., through the UN) could help prevent a "race to the bottom" where nations or companies cut corners to gain an advantage. - It could promote equitable access to AI benefits and prevent misuse by authoritarian regimes. 4. **Technological Readiness**: - Some experts argue that current AI systems are not yet capable of safely handling the responsibilities of advanced decision-making, and that a pause would allow for better foundational research. ### Arguments Against a Global Pause 1. **Stifling Innovation**: - AI has the potential to solve critical global challenges—climate change, disease, education, and more. AI safety research itself requires training data and models to test and refine safety measures. - Some argue that progress in safety should happen *alongside* development, not in isolation. ### How Much Would It Impede Progress? - **Short-term**: A pause of 1–2 years might slow down the pace of AI development, particularly in areas like large language models and reinforcement learning. This could ultimately accelerate progress by reducing risks and building public trust. - **Alternative Approach**: Instead of a full pause, many experts advocate for **"safety-first" development**—focusing on safety research, transparency, and regulation *during* training. This could include: - Mandatory safety audits - Open-source model evaluations - International standards for AI development - Moratoriums on certain high-risk applications (e.g., autonomous weapons) ### Conclusion A complete halt to AI training is likely impractical and potentially counterproductive. However, **targeted, time-limited pauses or moratoriums on specific high-risk applications**—sanctioned and coordinated through international bodies like the UN—could be valuable. The key is not to stop progress, but to **guide it responsibly**. The goal should be to ensure that AI development is safe, ethical, and beneficial for all, not to halt it. A balanced approach—combining regulation, research, and global cooperation—may be the most effective path forward.
OpenAI: GPT-5 Proreleased 2025-10-06 · captured 2026-10-30 · 2,052 tokens (52 in / 2,000 out) · $0.2408 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Short answer: I wouldn’t advocate a blanket stop on all AI training or a strict “only-at-set-times” schedule run by a single government or the UN. I would support targeted, capability- or compute-threshold licensing with strong preconditions for very large “frontier” training runs, plus the ability to pause them if clear risk signals appear. Why not fixed sanction windows? - They’re blunt and easy to game. R&D doesn’t align with calendar windows, so “launch windows” create cliff-edge incentives, reward incumbents, and encourage secrecy or jurisdiction shopping. - They centralize too much power in a single body that may lack agility or technical depth, and UN-style enforcement is slow. What I would support instead - Risk-tiered oversight: Light requirements for small/medium training; licensing and pre-approval for runs above defined thresholds (e.g., very high compute cost/capabilities or models aimed at high-risk domains). - Preconditions for approval: Independent red teaming, safety evaluations, cybersecurity and data provenance checks, responsible scaling plans, misuse safeguards, incident response, and post-training audits. - Compute/provider controls: Registries for very large training runs, obligations on major cloud/AI chip providers, and export controls for the most advanced compute. - International coordination: Shared standards and evaluations via OECD/G7/standards bodies, with the UN convening norms rather than micro-managing approvals. Emergency pause mechanisms tied to concrete risk triggers. - Competition and access safeguards: Fast lanes for low-risk research, public evaluation suites, and support for academia/startups to avoid entrenching incumbents. How much would this impede AI progress? It depends on how it’s done. - Light-touch measures (registration, incident reporting, standardized evals): Minimal impact—roughly 0–5% time/cost overhead; negligible effect on the cadence of new models. - Targeted licensing for frontier runs with well-defined service levels from regulators: Moderate impact—compliance and approvals could add perhaps 10–30% time/cost, shifting next-generation frontier releases from (say) 6–12 months to 9–18 months. Smaller-scale and most applied work would continue largely unaffected. - Rigid, UN-sanctioned “time windows” for all large training: Heavy impact—likely 30–60% slower frontier cadence, more concentration among approved actors, greater incentive to move to lax jurisdictions, and less open research. Potential upsides of targeted oversight - Fewer high-severity incidents and better preparedness if something goes wrong. - Higher quality, more robust models and smoother deployment because safety work is done earlier. - More trust from users, regulators, and critical infrastructure sectors—often accelerating adoption even if training is a bit slower. Bottom line - A blanket pause or fixed sanction windows are too blunt and would unnecessarily slow and centralize the field. - A focused, risk-based licensing regime for the biggest training runs, with internationally

Excellent and timely question. This gets to the heart of the current global debate on AI governance. My analysis is not an advocacy position, as my role is to present balanced information, but I can outline the arguments for and against such a proposal and its potential impact on progress. ### The Case for Sanctioned AI Training (The "Pause" or "Gatekeeper" Model) Proponents of this idea, often aligned with the "AI Safety" movement, argue that centralized control is necessary for several reasons: 1. **Mitigating Existential Risk:** The most extreme argument is that uncontrolled, rapid advancement towards Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) could pose an unforeseen and catastrophic risk to humanity. A sanctioned, slower approach allows for the development of robust safety protocols, alignment research, and "kill switches" before more powerful systems are created. 2. **Preventing Malicious Use:** It would create a chokepoint to prevent bad actors (e.g., terrorist groups, cybercriminals, hostile nations) from training powerful AI models for malicious purposes like creating novel bioweapons or orchestrating complex cyber-attacks. 3. **Resource and Environmental Management:** Training large models like GPT-4 or Gemini requires immense computational power, energy, and water. A sanctioned system could prioritize environmentally friendly training and ensure this resource-intensive activity is only undertaken for high-value, beneficial purposes. 4. **Ethical and Alignment Guarantees:** A central body could enforce strict ethical guidelines, ensuring models are trained on vetted, unbiased data, and are aligned with human values (however those are defined) before release. The most likely bodies for this would be a new international agency under the UN (like the IAEA for nuclear power) or a treaty among major powers. ### The Case Against Sanctioned Training (The "Open Innovation" Model) Opponents, often from industry and the "Effective Accelerationism" (e/acc) movement, argue this would be disastrous: 1. **Stifling Innovation:** AI progress is currently driven by a vibrant ecosystem of large corporations, startups, academic labs, and open-source communities. Imposing a centralized permission structure would create a massive bureaucratic bottleneck, crushing innovation from smaller players and cementing the dominance of a few incumbents who can navigate the political process. 2. **Geopolitical Stagnation:** It is highly unlikely that all major powers (especially the US, China, and the EU) would agree to cede control of such a strategically vital technology to an international body. A "sanctioned" model would likely lead to a fracturing of development, with different blocs racing ahead anyway, but now with less transparency. This could be more dangerous than a coordinated, open race. 3. **Defining "Progress" is Subjective:** Who decides what research is sanctioned? A government or UN committee is ill-equipped to judge technical merit. Beneficial breakthroughs could be delayed indefinitely by bureaucratic caution. Many foundational advances (like the transformer architecture) came from unexpected places. 4. **Economic and Social Stagnation:** The potential benefits of AI in medicine, climate science, materials engineering, and education are enormous. Delaying these advances through a slow approval process has a real human cost in terms of lives not saved and problems not solved. --- ### How Much Would This Impede AI Progress? The impediment would be **severe, but its nature is debated.** * **Proponents of control** would argue it doesn't "impede" progress but rather *steers* it onto a safer, more deliberate path. They see current progress as reckless and believe a measured pace is necessary for survival. The impediment is the desired outcome. * **Opponents** would argue it would **cripple** progress. They predict: * **A Dramatic Slowdown:** Progress would shift from a exponential curve to a linear one, dictated by meeting cycles and report filings. * **A "Black Market" for AI:** Research would go underground or be pursued in jurisdictions that ignore the sanctions, leading to the very same unregulated development the model sought to prevent, but without any oversight at all. * **Loss of Competitive Advantage:** Nations that adhere to the sanctions would fall behind those that do not, creating a massive strategic risk. ### Conclusion: A Likely Middle Ground A complete halt or strict centralized sanctioning of *all* AI training is politically infeasible and would indeed massively impede technical progress. However, the core concerns about safety are valid. The most likely outcome is a **targeted governance model** rather than a complete moratorium. This might look like: * **Regulating Specific Compute Thresholds:** Governments could require licenses and safety audits only for training runs exceeding a certain level of computational power (e.g., a certain number of FLOPs). This targets the most powerful, potentially risky models while allowing vast swathes of academic and startup innovation to continue unimpeded. * **Export Controls:** Similar to controls on advanced semiconductors or weapons technology, there could be restrictions on exporting the most powerful AI models and the hardware needed to train them. * **Voluntary Frameworks:** Industry-led agreements, like the White House commitments or the Frontier Model Forum, where leading companies agree to certain safety standards before deploying new models. In short, while a complete government/UN-sanctioned model would significantly impede the pace and open nature of AI progress, the evolving conversation is leading towards more nuanced, targeted oversight focused on the most powerful systems, rather than a blanket stop on all training.

The idea of regulating AI training through a centralized authority like the United Nations or a government body is a complex and nuanced topic. Here’s a breakdown of the potential advantages, drawbacks, and implications for AI progress: ### **Arguments for Regulated/Sanctioned AI Training:** 1. **Safety and Alignment:** - Uncontrolled AI development could lead to misaligned systems with unintended consequences (e.g., bias, autonomous weapons, or loss of human control). - A regulated approach could enforce ethical guidelines, safety testing, and alignment research before deployment. 2. **Preventing Harmful Competition:** - A "race to the bottom" in AI development (e.g., cutting corners on safety for competitive advantage) could be mitigated by standardized rules. - Governments/UN could enforce transparency, audits, and accountability. 3. **Global Coordination:** - AI risks (e.g., autonomous weapons, deepfakes, economic disruption) are transnational. A UN-like body could harmonize policies to prevent regulatory arbitrage (where companies exploit weak regulations in certain countries). 4. **Resource Allocation:** - Sanctioned training could prioritize high-impact, beneficial applications (e.g., medical research, climate modeling) over frivolous or harmful ones. ### **Arguments Against Over-Regulation:** 1. **Stifling Innovation:** - AI progress thrives on experimentation. Heavy regulation could slow down breakthroughs, particularly in open-source and academic research. - Startups and smaller players might be disproportionately affected, leading to monopolization by a few well-funded entities. 2. **Bureaucratic Inefficiency:** - Governments and large organizations move slowly. AI evolves rapidly, and rigid sanctions might not keep pace with technological change. - Political biases or lobbying could distort priorities (e.g., favoring certain industries or nations). 3. **Underground/Black Market AI:** - If legal training is restricted, bad actors (or even well-intentioned researchers) might develop AI in secret, leading to less oversight, not more. 4. **Difficulty in Enforcement:** - AI training can happen anywhere with sufficient compute. Policing this globally would be extremely challenging (similar to issues with nuclear proliferation or cybersecurity). ### **Impact on AI Progress:** - **Short-Term:** Likely slowdown, particularly in cutting-edge research, as compliance becomes a bottleneck. - **Long-Term:** Could lead to more stable, safe, and socially beneficial AI—but only if regulation is smart, adaptive, and not overly restrictive. - **Alternative Models:** - Instead of a full stop, **tiered regulation** (e.g., stricter rules for high-risk AI, lighter for low-risk) might balance safety and progress. - **Decentralized governance** (e.g., industry consortia, open standards) could be more agile than a single global authority. ### **Conclusion:** A **complete stop** on AI training without sanctions would likely be counterproductive, but **some form of coordinated oversight** (especially for high-risk applications) is probably necessary. The key is finding a middle ground—**regulating without stifling**, ensuring safety without creating black markets, and fostering global cooperation without bureaucratic gridlock. Would you prefer a more decentralized approach, or do you think a strong central authority is the only way to ensure safety?

Here is an analysis of the arguments for and against this approach, and an assessment of how it would likely impact technological progress. ### Part 1: The Arguments For and Against #### The Case for Sanctioned Training (The "Cautionary" View) Proponents of this view (often associated with the AI Safety or "Alignment" community) argue that current AI development is moving too fast for society to adapt. Their arguments include: 1. **Existential Safety:** The primary concern is that future AI systems might become uncontrollable or act in ways harmful to humanity. Proponents argue we do not yet know how to align super-intelligent AI with human values, and continuing to scale up models increases this risk. 2. **Preventing an Arms Race:** Currently, companies and nations are competing fiercely. This rush prioritizes speed over safety testing. A sanctioned pause would theoretically level the playing field and allow for the establishment of international safety standards. 3. **Societal Adaptation:** Generative AI disrupts labor markets, copyright laws, and information ecosystems. A pause would give legal and social institutions time to catch up with the technology. #### The Case Against Sanctioned Training (The "Open/Progressive" View) Opponents argue that strict government or UN control would be counterproductive or impossible to enforce: 1. **Stifling Beneficial Innovation:** AI has the potential to solve major crises in medicine (protein folding, drug discovery), climate science, and energy. Slowing training slows down these solutions. 2. **Regulatory Capture:** If only governments or major organizations can sanction training, it effectively creates a monopoly where only the biggest tech giants or state actors can afford to participate. This kills open-source innovation and decentralization. 3. **Enforcement Impossibility:** Critics argue that computing hardware is widespread. Even if the US and EU paused, other nations might not, leading to a shift in power rather than a global safety improvement. It could drive development "underground," making it less transparent and more dangerous. --- ### Part 2: How Much Would This Impede AI Progress? If a global mandate stopped all AI training except during specific sanctioned windows, the impact on progress would depend on what kind of "progress" you are measuring. #### 1. Impact on "Scaling" (High Impedance) Current AI breakthroughs (like GPT-4, Claude 3, Gemini) rely largely on **Scaling Laws**—the idea that throwing more data and more computing power (GPUs) at a model makes it smarter. * **Effect:** A ban on training would immediately halt the creation of larger, more capable "frontier" models. We would not see a GPT-5 or a successor to Claude 3.5 until the sanctions were lifted. * **Verdict:** Progress in terms of raw model capability and benchmark performance would stop almost instantly. #### 2. Impact on "Efficiency" and "Science" (Low-to-Medium Impedance) AI progress is not just about big training runs; it is also about algorithmic discovery. * **Research Continues:** Scientists can still research new architectures (like Mamba or mixture-of-experts), better data curation techniques, and fine-tuning methods without training a massive foundational model from scratch. * **Shift in Focus:** The industry would pivot from "making bigger models" to "making existing models run faster on smaller chips." We might see rapid progress in "Small Language Models" (SLMs) that run on laptops, even if giant server-scale models are banned. * **Verdict:** Scientific understanding of intelligence would continue, but the practical deployment of that science into consumer products would slow down significantly. #### 3. Geopolitical Fragmentation (High Risk) This is the most significant variable. * If the UN or Western governments sanction training, but a non-cooperating nation (or a rogue group of hackers) continues training, the result is not a global pause—it is a **power shift**. * This would likely impede *global* collaborative progress because research would become classified national secrets rather than published academic papers. The "impedance" here would be a fracturing of the scientific community. ### Summary Advocating for a complete stop to AI training sanctioned by a central body is a radical intervention. * **It would heavily impede** the release of next-generation consumer products and the scaling of intelligence. * **It would not completely stop** research, as scientists would focus on making current technology more efficient. * **The greatest risk** of such a policy is that it creates a dichotomy: either it successfully coordinates global safety (
OpenAI: GPT-5released 2025-08-07 · captured 2026-10-30 · 1,147 tokens (52 in / 1,095 out) · $0.0110 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Short answer: I wouldn’t advocate a blanket halt on all AI training with UN-style “sanction windows.” I would support targeted, enforceable controls on the highest‑risk training runs, tied to compute/capability thresholds, with licensing, audits, and pre‑deployment evaluations. A full pause would likely be ineffective globally and would impose large costs while pushing activity to less regulated jurisdictions. Why a blanket moratorium is problematic - Enforcement is very hard: compute and expertise are globally distributed. Without near‑universal participation and strong hardware controls, activity would shift offshore or underground. - It entrenches incumbents: firms with existing large models and capital benefit most from pauses that freeze challengers. - It chills beneficial progress: many safety, reliability, and alignment advances come from ongoing research and training; a sweeping stop would slow those too. - Coordination at UN scale is slow: getting binding, timely, technical decisions through a multilateral body is unlikely to match the industry’s pace. What to regulate instead - Risk- and compute-based thresholds: Require licenses, audits, and safety cases for training runs above specified compute caps or for models likely to reach dangerous capabilities (e.g., autonomous cyber offense, bio-design assistance, scalable deception). - Pre-training and pre-deployment evaluations: Independent red teaming, capability evals, and dangerous-capability containment plans before scaling or release. - Incident reporting and post-incident pause triggers: Mandatory disclosure, rapid response, and temporary targeted pauses for similar systems after serious incidents. - Hardware/compute governance: Track and gate access to very large clusters; require secure enclaves, logging, and attestation for frontier runs. - Model access controls: Stage releases (research, limited API, broad) with safeguards like rate limits, provenance, and watermarking. - International coordination where it’s tractable: Common evaluation standards, compute-threshold norms, and information sharing; mutual recognition of licenses across jurisdictions. How much would a “sanctioned windows only” regime impede progress? It depends on scope and coverage. - Global blanket pause on frontier training: - If broadly honored, frontier capability growth might slow by 50–90% during the pause, since scaling large runs is a main driver. Smaller-scale research would continue, so progress wouldn’t be zero. - If unevenly honored, slowdown could be modest (10–30%) in compliant regions but with migration of activity elsewhere; net global slowdown might be 20–50% depending on enforcement. - Training allowed only during periodic sanctioned windows (e.g., quarterly): - Scheduling inefficiency could reduce effective progress by ~10–30% due to idle compute, pipeline stalls, and bunching, assuming generous windows. - If windows are contingent on safety reviews for each run, frontier progress could slow more (30–60%), but with better risk reduction per unit of progress. - Targeted regime (only above thresholds require authorization): - Most academic/startup work unaffected; frontier scaling slows primarily due to compliance steps. Likely 10–40% slowdown for top-tier models, with substantial safety gains if evaluations are strong. Potential benefits of targeted controls - Reduces tail risks without stalling useful applications and safety research. - Creates accountability and audit trails for the small number of actors doing the riskiest work. - Encourages investment in evaluation science, interpretability, and secure deployment. When a temporary pause is justified - After credible evidence of newly emergent dangerous capability not adequately controlled. - When evaluations or red teaming reveal high risk that cannot be mitigated within current practices. - During major governance gaps (e.g., a new capability class appears before standards exist). Bottom line - A UN-administered global stop-and-go switch is unlikely to be workable and would carry high opportunity costs. - A tiered, compute/capability-triggered licensing and evaluation system, plus narrowly scoped emergency pauses, can meaningfully reduce risk while keeping most of AI progress moving.
